## business-model / 06_value-chain.md

_Source: `06_value-chain.md`_

# Value Chain Position — NU

**Regime and basis.** Nu Holdings Ltd. is a Cayman-incorporated foreign private issuer listed on the NYSE; the annual filing is a Form 20-F, not a 10-K. Reporting standard IFRS as issued by the IASB, reporting currency US dollars, fiscal year ends 31 December. Operating currencies are the Brazilian real, Mexican peso and Colombian peso. Tradable line referenced: **NYSE: NU, Class A ordinary shares, USD** [`FY25 Form 20-F, cover page`].

**What this agent is reading.** Upstream `02_business-identity.md` establishes the offering: a branchless digital consumer bank whose customer pays for credit, with 85.2% of FY2025 revenue arriving as interest income and gains net of losses on financial instruments [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — "Interest Income and Gains net of Losses on Financial Instruments"`]. For a bank the value chain is not made of physical parts. Its main input is **money** (deposits), and its other real inputs are the **card network** it must license to issue cards, the **cloud and network infrastructure** it runs on, and the **rails** the central bank owns. Those are the stages scored below.

**One source is deliberately excluded.** The 15-page deep-dive memo dated 30 Aug 2026 sitting in the pool is a prior engine output, not evidence. It is treated as a §4 tier-9 user note and nothing in this report is taken from it.

---

## 1. Stages Occupied

| Value Chain Stage | Company Role (1 sentence) | Bargaining Power vs Upstream | Bargaining Power vs Downstream | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Funding / deposit-taking** (the money input) | Raises retail deposits directly from its own app customers and uses them to fund the loan book. | **Strong** — it pays 88% of the local interbank rate for deposits, three percentage points less than a year earlier, and holds a loan-to-deposit ratio of 35%, so it does not need the next deposit and can price accordingly. | *n/a — internal.* The money moves to Nu's own lending book, not to an outside buyer. | US$41.9bn of deposits at 31 Dec 2025, "all of which were acquired directly, without the use of third-party brokers or intermediaries" [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — "Low Cost of Funding"`]; cost of deposits 88% of the interbank rate, "essentially unchanged from last quarter and 3 percentage points lower than a year ago", loan-to-deposit ratio 35% [`Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), CFO prepared remarks`] |
| **Underwriting and lending** (the manufacturer) | Sets the credit limit and the interest rate, and keeps the loss on its own balance sheet. | **Strong** — its funding is 90.5% retail deposits priced below the benchmark; it is not dependent on a wholesale lender. | **Mid** — it sets its own rates and grew the book 37% year on year, but a Brazilian statute caps the total interest and charges on revolving and installment card credit at the original debt amount, and a separate cap applies to INSS payroll loans. | Deposits US$41,925.1m vs borrowings and financing US$4,398.2m → deposits are 90.5% of interest-bearing funding [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Borrowings and Financing`; `CIQ Financials→Industry Specific, FY2025 — vendor export`]; credit portfolio US$39.4bn, +37% year on year [`Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), CFO prepared remarks`]; Law 14,690/2023 and CMN Res. 5,112/2023 cap total interest and charges at the original debt amount [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Regulatory Overview, Brazil`] |
| **Card issuing on a licensed network** | Issues Mastercard-branded credit and prepaid cards; Mastercard authorises every transaction and sets the interchange rate Nu earns. | **Mid** — Mastercard is named as a sole source and "may increase the fees that they charge", but in December 2024 Nu renewed and extended the partnership *with volume-linked incentives paid to Nu*, the trademark licences carry no consideration, and the network cost line fell from 0.95% to 0.68% of revenue over two years. | **Weak** — Nu does not set the price it earns from merchants: "These fees are set by Mastercard and paid by the merchants who accept our cards", and BCB Res. 246 caps prepaid interchange at 0.7%, which the filing says already hit fee income. | "certain third-party service providers, including Mastercard, are the sole source or one of a limited number of sources of the services they provide for us" and "from time to time, the payment schemes may increase the fees that they charge" [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D risk factors`]; "In December 2024, Nu renewed and extended its long-term partnership with Mastercard, including incentive mechanisms linked to prepaid and credit transaction volume performance" [`FY25 Form 20-F, Consolidated Financial Statements, note on payables to credit card network`]; "No consideration is due to Mastercard under this agreement" (all three licences) [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 10.C — Material Contracts`]; "BCB Res. 246 imposes a maximum interchange fee of 0.7% on all prepaid card transactions in Brazil, which impacted our fee and commission income" [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Regulation of Credit Cards and Interchange Fees`] |
| **Technology and network infrastructure** (bought in) | Runs entirely on third-party cloud, third-party data centres and a single named telecom link into Brazil's interbank network. | **Weak** — AWS is the "primary" cloud provider and the filing states plainly that Nu "cannot easily switch our operations to another on similar terms cloud provider"; the connection to Brazil's RSFN interbank network depends on one named provider, RTM. | *n/a — internal.* This is a cost input, not something Nu resells. | "We partnered with Amazon Web Services to be our primary third-party cloud infrastructure provider" [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Cloud-Based Architecture`]; "we cannot easily switch our operations to another on similar terms cloud provider" [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D risk factors`]; "our infrastructure services and connection to the National Brazilian Financial System Network, or 'RSFN,' depends on the infrastructure of Market Telecommunications Network... or 'RTM,' a datacenter and link provider" [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D risk factors`] |
| **Direct-to-consumer distribution** (the brand and the only channel) | Owns the whole customer relationship through its own app — no branches, no agents, no deposit brokers, no loan intermediaries. | **Strong** — there is no upstream party in this stage. Nu removed the intermediary rather than paying one, and says this is why it can undercut on payroll loans. | **Mid** — 139 million customers with no contract, no minimum term and no switching penalty, so power rests on habit and price rather than on lock-in. | "lower interest rates compared to competitors due to cost efficiency as we do not use intermediaries to distribute our product. According to the Central Bank of Brazil's interest rates statistics report released in December 2025, Nubank already had one of the lowest interest rates for payroll loans" [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — payroll loans`]; no long-term customer contract is disclosed anywhere in the filing [`05_customer-geography.md`, §3, citing `FY25 Form 20-F`] |
| **Agent / marketplace for third-party products** (insurance, investments, remittance, top-ups) | Sells other firms' products to its own base and takes a commission. | **Mid** — it brings the customers, but the products are made by others; no exclusivity is disclosed. | **Strong** — it controls the shelf and the customer. | Insurance commission US$35.5m and other fee and commission income US$199.2m in FY2025, together 1.5% of total revenue [`FY25 Form 20-F, Note 6(b)`]; "Zero-Spread Multi-currency account, in partnership with Wise, supporting deposits and spending in over 40 currencies" [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B`] |

Bargaining power bands:
- **Strong:** Company sets price, dictates terms, has alternatives
- **Mid:** Negotiated outcomes, no extreme leverage either way
- **Weak:** Price-taker, terms imposed, few alternatives

---

## 2. Input Cost Pass-Through

The dominant input cost for this business is the rate it pays for deposits. It is not a raw material, but it behaves exactly like one: an outside benchmark moves, and Nu's cost of goods moves with it. Brazil's policy rate went from 10.50% on 8 May 2024 to 15.00% on 18 June 2025 and was still 15.00% at the date of the annual report — a 450 basis point rise in the price of Nu's main input inside about thirteen months [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Brazilian macroeconomic environment`].

| Axis | What it asks | Verdict | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Contractual pass-through** | Is there an escalator, indexed-pricing, or cost-plus clause that moves price automatically? | **Partial, and it runs the wrong way on the input side.** Nu's *cost* is contractually indexed — deposits are priced as a percentage of the local interbank rate, the Colombian syndicated loan is indexed to IBR, the margin loan to CME Term SOFR, the DFC facility at SOFR + 2.90%. On the *revenue* side there is **no** disclosed escalator: credit card and personal loan rates are set by Nu at its own discretion, and interchange is set by a third party (Mastercard) and capped by the regulator (BCB Res. 246, 0.7% on prepaid). So the input re-prices automatically and the output does not. | `Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), CFO prepared remarks` (cost of deposits as % of interbank rate); `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Borrowings and Financing` (IBR, CME Term SOFR, SOFR + 2.90%); `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Regulation of Credit Cards and Interchange Fees`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — "Fees and Commission Income"` |
| **Realised recovery** | Of a given input-cost increase, how much did the company actually recover — through price, mix, hedging, re-sourcing, or a cost programme? | **Roughly 40% recovered on the deposit-price lever alone; more than fully recovered at the spread level by Q2 FY26; but not recovered inside the FY2025 annual accounts, where gross margin still fell 361bp.** All three numbers are shown below because they answer different questions and a single number here would mislead. | Derived from `FY25 Form 20-F, "Statement of Income Data"` and `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Interest and other financial expenses`; `Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026)`; `Q3 FY25 earnings call transcript (13 Nov 2025)` |

**The realised-recovery arithmetic, shown.**

*Step 1 — size the gross, pre-mitigation shock, separating rate from volume.* Interest expense on deposits rose US$1,534.3m, or 65.5%, in FY2025, so the FY2024 base was US$2,342.4m (1,534.3 ÷ 0.655) and the FY2025 figure US$3,876.7m. Over the same year deposits grew 45.3%, from US$28.9bn to US$41.9bn [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Interest and other financial expenses`]. Had the unit rate not moved at all, the volume growth alone would have carried the expense to US$3,403.5m (2,342.4 × 1.453). The residual — **US$473.2m — is the part caused purely by the higher rate**, and that is 3.00% of FY2025 total revenue of US$15,774.8m, or **300 basis points of revenue** [`FY25 Form 20-F, "Statement of Income Data"`]. *Basis flag (§15): the 45.3% deposit growth is a point-in-time year-end-to-year-end change, while the interest expense is a full-year flow. The split is therefore approximate, not exact; the filing does not disclose average deposit balances.*

*Step 2 — measure the mitigation Nu actually applied.* It cut the price it pays depositors relative to the benchmark: the consolidated cost of deposits was 88% of the interbank rate in Q2 FY26, "3 percentage points lower than a year ago" [`Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), CFO prepared remarks`], having been 91% a year before that and then 89% and 87% at intermediate points [`Q3 FY25 earnings call transcript (13 Nov 2025), CFO Q&A`; `Q4 FY25 earnings call transcript (25 Feb 2026), CFO prepared remarks`]. Three percentage points of a 15.00% Brazilian benchmark is about 0.45 percentage points of absolute deposit rate; on the US$41.9bn FY2025 deposit balance that is roughly **US$189m a year, or about 40% of the US$473.2m rate shock** (188.6 ÷ 473.2 = 39.9%). *Labelled derivation — inference, not from filings, on the aggregation: the 3pp figure is consolidated across three countries while 15.00% is the Brazilian rate only (Brazil held US$36.4bn of US$45.3bn deposits at Q2 FY26), and the 3pp change is a Q2 FY26 year-on-year measure applied to a FY2025 year-end balance. Read it as order of magnitude, not to the dollar.*

*Step 3 — check where it landed.* Two answers, and they differ by period. **In the FY2025 annual accounts, more than the whole rate shock landed.** Interest and other financial expenses rose from 24.61% of revenue (2,834.9 ÷ 11,517.0) to 29.02% (4,578.7 ÷ 15,774.8), a 441bp drag against a 300bp rate component; gross profit margin fell 361 basis points, from 45.61% (5,252.8 ÷ 11,517.0) to 42.00% (6,625.0 ÷ 15,774.8) [`FY25 Form 20-F, "Statement of Income Data"`]. Note what makes up the difference: of the 441bp, roughly 300bp is the rate and roughly 141bp is Nu deliberately holding more deposits than it lends out — a loan-to-deposit ratio of 35% is a balance-sheet choice, not a cost it failed to recover. **By Q2 FY26 the spread had more than recovered:** net interest margin (the gap between what it earns on loans and what it pays on deposits) reached 22.9% and risk-adjusted net interest margin (that gap after credit losses) 12.4%, up from 9.5% the prior quarter, both records on management's framework [`Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), CFO prepared remarks`]. *Basis warning: management restated its operating metrics onto a new managerial, non-IFRS framework in Q4 FY25 and back-cast it to Q1 2021, so a NIM figure quoted from a Q3 FY25 call (17.7% falling to 17.3%) is not on the same basis as the 22.9% quoted for Q2 FY26. Do not subtract one from the other. The like-for-like statements used above are the ones management itself made year-on-year (cost of deposits 3pp lower) and quarter-on-quarter within the new framework (9.5% → 12.4%).*

**The mechanisms the filings actually disclose, and how well each worked.**

Nu recovers input cost through four levers, and the filings let three of them be measured. **First, re-pricing its own deposits down** — the 91% → 88% path above, worth roughly 40% of the rate shock, and it is a *price* action with a visible volume cost: management "lowering deposit yields in Mexico and Colombia... with some significant changes implemented only now in early July 2025" [`Q2 FY25 earnings call transcript (14 Aug 2025), CFO prepared remarks`], after which Mexican deposits fell in Q1 FY26 and again in Q2 FY26, which management called a deliberate "deposit optimization strategy" [`Q1 FY26 earnings call transcript (14 May 2026)`; `Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026)`]. **Second, asset mix** — shifting the book toward unsecured lending, which grew 45% year on year to US$10.3bn against secured at 30%, plus what the CFO calls "the deliberate risk expansions we made"; management attributes 178bp of the quarter's risk-adjusted NIM expansion to credit income [`Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), CFO prepared remarks`]. **Third, the card take rate.** Despite the 0.7% prepaid interchange cap that the filing says "impacted our fee and commission income", credit and prepaid card income rose 24.6% to US$1,720.3m on card purchase volume growth of 17% — implying the money Nu keeps per unit of card volume **rose about 6.5%** (1.246 ÷ 1.17 − 1) [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Total revenue and Total fee and commission income`]. *Contradicting figure adjudicated by name (§3): the same MD&A paragraph also states "total purchase volume grew 29%". On that base the take rate would have **fallen 3.4%** (1.246 ÷ 1.29 − 1). The filing does not reconcile the two volume figures; the 17% is the one it attaches directly to the card-income line and is the matched basis, so it is used as primary — but the direction of this one measure is not settled by the pool, and it should not be quoted without the 29% alongside it.* **Fourth, the network cost line held.** Payments and network costs — which include the fee paid to Mastercard — went US$76.2m → US$83.6m → US$107.6m across FY2023–FY2025, which is 0.95% → 0.73% → 0.68% of total revenue [`FY25 Form 20-F, Consolidated Financial Statements, note 6(d) Transactional expenses`; revenue from `"Statement of Income Data"`]. The sole-source supplier that the risk factor says can raise its fees has, on this measure, taken a *smaller* share of Nu's revenue each year. What the filing does not let anyone see: the Mastercard-only portion of that line, because it also carries boleto issuance, Apple Pay fees, partner-bank ATM withdrawals and chargeback restatements.

Distinguishing the two kinds of recovery: the deposit re-pricing and the card take rate are **price pass-through** — the depositor gets less and the merchant-funded fee per unit holds or rises. The mix shift into unsecured lending and the falling network-cost share are **cost absorption and mix** — Nu's own economics improved rather than the customer paying more. That matters for durability, because the mix lever runs out (unsecured lending is already 26% of a book that grew 37%, and its losses arrive later), while a re-priced deposit base tends to stay re-priced.

**What must not be written downstream.** There is **no contractual escalator on Nu's revenue**. That is a fact about contracts. It is *not* a finding that Nu cannot recover input cost — the measured recovery on the deposit lever alone is roughly 40%, and the spread measures had fully recovered by Q2 FY26. Anyone shortening this section must carry the word "contractual", or quote the realised-recovery numbers instead.

**Supplier / input concentration (quantified where disclosed).**

| Input | Concentration | Quantified? |
|---|---|---|
| **Money (funding)** | Retail deposits are **90.5%** of interest-bearing funding: US$41,925.1m of deposits against US$4,398.2m of borrowings and financing (total indebtedness US$4,427.4m including US$29.2m of leases). No single depositor is named or material; the base is 139 million retail customers. All of it "acquired directly, without the use of third-party brokers or intermediaries". | **Yes** [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Borrowings and Financing` and `Item 3.D`; `CIQ Financials→Industry Specific, FY2025 — vendor export`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — "Low Cost of Funding"`] |
| **Card network** | **Single scheme, named sole source.** Mastercard authorises every credit and prepaid card transaction, and the card book is US$26bn of the US$39.4bn portfolio (66%) at Q2 FY26. This is the one genuinely single-sourced, genuinely material input. | **Partly.** The dependency is disclosed and the card book is sized, but the filing never states what share of COGS or purchases Mastercard represents — the payments-and-network line (US$107.6m, 0.68% of revenue) is an upper bound that also contains several other vendors [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D risk factors`; `Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026)`; `FY25 Form 20-F, note 6(d)`] |
| **Cloud** | AWS is the "primary" third-party cloud infrastructure provider and the filing admits Nu "cannot easily switch". A material single-source dependency by its own description. | **No.** The filing discloses **no** cost, no share of operating expenses, and no contract term for AWS. Do not invent one [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B` and `Item 3.D`] |
| **Interbank network link** | RTM is named as the single provider of the connection to Brazil's RSFN. | **No** — named, never sized [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D risk factors`] |
| **Wholesale credit** | Small and shrinking as a dependency. The DFC facility for Nu Colombia was cut from US$150m committed (US$50m drawn) to **US$100m committed and fully undisbursed** after Nu prepaid the US$50m during FY2025; Nu Holdings guarantees it, and it carries capital, funding, liquidity, profitability and leverage covenants, in compliance at 31 Dec 2025. | **Yes** [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Borrowings and Financing`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Consolidated Financial Statements, Covenants and Guarantees note`] |

The filing discloses **no** largest-supplier or top-3-supplier share of purchases anywhere. That figure does not exist in this pool and must not be estimated.

| United States International Development Finance Corporation (DFC) | *unlisted in the export* | Creditor — Specialized Finance | Nu Colombia S.A. | Nu's own filing (`2026 Form 20-F`) | A lender, not a trading counterparty. The filing shows the facility is now **US$100m committed and undisbursed** after Nu prepaid the drawn US$50m, so it is optionality, not a dependency. |

- **Concentration of disclosure.** Twelve entities are named across eleven relationship rows: nine are genuine outside parties, three are Nu's own subsidiaries (Nu Pagamentos, Nu BN México, Nu Colombia) and are excluded from every count above. Only three of the nine outside parties carry a listing, across three exchanges (ENXTPA, LSE, NYSE). The intra-group share of rows is **0.0%**, so there is no related-party trading signal here to hand to `capital-allocation-governance` — the group entities appear only as Nu's own side of outside relationships.
- **Where the spend clusters.** One cluster matters: **Transaction and Payment Processing Services**, with three outside parties (Edenred SE, Wise Group plc, MasterCard International). That is the bargaining fact in this table — Nu's disclosed outside spend concentrates in payment rails it does not own. A second cluster, Investment Banking and Brokerage (Computershare, Numis), is corporate housekeeping, not an operating input.
- **The honest limit.** The export names relationships and nothing more. It states explicitly, for every one of these, that "how much of [the counterparty's] revenue Nu actually accounts for is not disclosed here" and "whether the relationship is contracted, sole-source, or easily switched is not disclosed here". The Mastercard sole-source finding in §1 comes from Nu's own 20-F risk factor, **not** from this export. No percentage in this report is derived from a name in this table.

---

## 3. Customer Pricing Power

Nu can raise price without losing volume, and the clearest proof is that it keeps choosing not to. The credit book grew 37% year on year to US$39.4bn while management held its underwriting framework unchanged, and the price it pays for its own funding fell three percentage points of the benchmark over the same year — so both sides of the spread moved in Nu's favour at once [`Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), CFO prepared remarks`]. Two explicit pricing actions inside the last 24 months are on the record, and both are **cuts**, not rises. On the deposit side, management began "lowering deposit yields in Mexico and Colombia... with some significant changes implemented only now in early July 2025", and the volume reaction was visible and accepted: Mexican deposits fell in Q1 FY26 and again in Q2 FY26, which the CFO described as an "ongoing deposit optimization strategy" that "continues to improve our cost of funding while maintaining ample liquidity with our loan-to-deposit ratio still at just 35%" [`Q2 FY25 earnings call transcript (14 Aug 2025)`; `Q1 FY26 earnings call transcript (14 May 2026)`; `Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026)`]. On the lending side, Nu competes by charging less: the central bank's own December 2025 rate statistics put it among the lowest payroll-loan rates in Brazil, which the filing attributes to cutting out the distributing intermediary rather than to accepting a thinner margin [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — payroll loans`]. The CEO states the trade-off directly: on the AI-driven price-optimisation work, "I wouldn't necessarily think in the short term that this will drive an improvement in funding costs because we might decide to reinvest all of those gains back into the customer... We're not in a mode of optimizing for net income" [`Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), CEO Q&A`]. The honest reading is that pricing power exists on the deposit side (measured: 3pp of benchmark reclaimed, with a tolerated deposit outflow in Mexico) and is **unproven by action** on the lending side, because Nu has not tested how much volume a rate rise would cost it. Two hard limits sit above it regardless: Brazilian law caps total interest and charges on revolving and installment card credit at the original debt amount, and the interchange Nu earns is set by Mastercard and capped by the central bank at 0.7% on prepaid [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Regulatory Overview, Brazil` and `Regulation of Credit Cards and Interchange Fees`].

---

## 4. Economic Control Verdict

**Mixed.**

Nu controls the input side of its economics almost completely: it manufactures its own funding from 139 million retail depositors, pays 88% of the local interbank rate for it, cut that by three percentage points in a year, lends out only 35 cents of every dollar it holds, and buys none of it through brokers or intermediaries — so no supplier of money has leverage over it, and the US$100m DFC facility, its only named outside credit line, is committed and undrawn [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — "Low Cost of Funding"` and `Borrowings and Financing`; `Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026)`]. On the output side it does not control the price of a fifth of what it earns and cannot fully control the rest: the fees behind US$1,720.3m of FY2025 card income are "set by Mastercard and paid by the merchants", the prepaid slice of them is capped at 0.7% by BCB Res. 246, and the interest on the card book is capped by statute at the original debt amount [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — "Fees and Commission Income"`, `Note 6(b)`, `Item 4.B — Regulatory Overview`]. The party actually setting the rules on that side is not a supplier or a customer but the regulator — and it can move fast: a change to FGTS rules "reduced new originations by more than half" in one quarter [`Q4 FY25 earnings call transcript (25 Feb 2026), CFO prepared remarks`]. Strong upstream, constrained downstream, is the definition of Mixed.

---

## 5. The Single Biggest Bargaining Risk

**Mastercard.** It is the named sole source for authorising every one of Nu's card transactions — the card book is US$26bn of a US$39.4bn portfolio (66%) — it sets the interchange rate that produced US$1,720.3m of FY2025 fee income, it "may increase the fees that they charge", and it can fine, suspend or terminate Nu's registration outright; the December 2024 renewal with volume-linked incentives blunts this today but does not remove it, because nothing in the filing discloses the term or the exit terms of that agreement [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D risk factors`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Note 6(b)`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Consolidated Financial Statements, payables to credit card network note`; `Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026)`].



---

## business-model / 07_business-quality.md

_Source: `07_business-quality.md`_

# Business Quality — NU

**Upstream inputs:** all present. `02_business-identity.md`, `03_segment-map.md`, `05_customer-geography.md` (required), plus `00_data-triage.md`, `10_external-dependency.md`, `11_capital-allocation-governance.md` were read. No upstream output is missing.

**Regime and basis.** Nu Holdings Ltd. (NYSE: NU, Class A ordinary shares, USD) is a Cayman-incorporated US SEC foreign private issuer; the annual filing is **Form 20-F**, not a 10-K. Reporting standard **IFRS as issued by the IASB**, reporting currency **US dollars**, fiscal year ends 31 December `[FY25 Form 20-F (filed 8 Apr 2026), cover page]`.

**Two bases are used below and they are kept apart, as the triage requires.** (a) **IFRS / audited**: total revenue, expected credit loss, net income, capital ratios, sensitivity tables. (b) **Managerial / non-IFRS, FX-neutral**: NIM, risk-adjusted NIM, ARPAC, cost to serve, efficiency ratio, "gross revenue", loan-to-deposit ratio — all company-defined measures introduced in Q4'25 and not equal to the audited figures (managerial gross revenue c. US$5.9bn in Q2'26 against IFRS total revenue of US$5,513.2m) `[Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), consolidated statement of profit or loss; Q2'26 investor deck (13 Aug 2026), Non-IFRS Financial Measures and Reconciliations, pp.30–33]`. (c) **Vendor (tier 5)**: Capital IQ ratio series, labelled as such every time. Each row below states which basis it is on.

**Not used as evidence:** `data/NU/NU_Holdings_Deep_Dive_15_Page_Memo_30_Aug_2026.pdf` is a prior engine output carrying its own scores. Per the triage caveat it is treated as a §4 tier-9 user note; it was not read for, and did not inform, any score here.

**Sector overlay applied: Bank / lender (`frameworks/SECTOR_OVERLAYS.md`) — factors *capital intensity*, *margin stability*, *cyclicality* and *commodity dependence* use the sector-specific lens, not the generic manufacturing one.** Concretely: capital intensity is read as **regulatory** capital intensity (CET1 / Tier 1 / CAR consumption and the loan-to-deposit ratio), not as property-and-equipment spending; margin stability is read as **NIM and risk-adjusted NIM stability across the credit cycle**, not gross-margin variance; cyclicality is read as **credit-cycle / NPL severity**, not volume cycle; commodity dependence is read as **rate-cycle / NIM sensitivity**, because this business has no physical commodity exposure at all. Where the overlay moves a score materially against the generic reading, the row says so and gives both numbers.

## 1. Quality Factor Table

Bands (repo-root `CLAUDE.md` §12): 0–20 very weak · 21–40 weak · 41–60 mixed/average · 61–80 strong · 81–100 very strong. **Every row is higher = better.** Six rows are reverse-mapped, meaning low intensity / dependence / cyclicality / rate-of-change earns a HIGH score; each is flagged in its label.

| Quality Factor | Score /100 | Evidence | Comment |
|---|---:|---|---|
| **Pricing power** *(higher = better)* | **45** | Price of the largest revenue line is set by statute, not by the company: Law 14,690/2023 with CMN Res. 5,112 and BCB Res. 365 (21 Dec 2023) provide that total interest and financial charges on revolving and instalment card credit "may not exceed the original amount of the debt financed" `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Revolving Credit and Interest Rate Regulations]`. Card interest was 38.0% of the Note-34 revenue base in FY2025 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 6(a), pp.F-30–F-31]`. Interchange is capped at 0.7% on prepaid and 0.5% on debit by BCB Res. 246, which the filing says "impacted our fee and commission income" `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Interchange Fees]`, and the rate itself is set by Mastercard, not Nu `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Fees and Commission Income]`. On the other side, funding cost was actively managed down: cost of deposits ran 90% / 91% / 89% / 87% / 88% / 88% of the local interbank rate from Q1'25 to Q2'26 `[Q2'26 investor deck (13 Aug 2026), Deposit Franchise, p.14 — managerial basis]`, and managerial NIM rose 18.8% → 22.9% over the same four quarters `[Q2'26 deck, p.15]`. | Mixed, and the two halves point opposite ways. Nu can lower what it pays; it largely cannot raise what it charges on 38% of revenue, and it does not set the fee on another 14.2%. The NIM rise is mostly **mix**, not price — the company's own quarter-on-quarter walk attributes +152bp then +178bp to Credit Income while the average loan-to-deposit ratio went 50% → 58%, i.e. more of the balance sheet moved into the high-yield book `[Q2'26 deck, p.16 — managerial basis]`. Being the low-cost producer is a cost advantage, not pricing power; the moat agent owns that distinction. |
| **Repeat / recurring revenue** *(higher = better)* | **72** | 85.2% of FY2025 total revenue was interest income and gains net of losses on financial instruments (US$13,434.7m of US$15,774.7m), fees 14.8% `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Operating Results]`. That interest accrues month after month on a revolving card and instalment book of US$39.4bn `[Q2'26 deck, p.13 — managerial basis]`, funded by US$45.3bn of deposits that were 98.9% non-time (on-demand / savings) at FY2025 `[CIQ Financials → Industry Specific export, FY2025 — vendor export]`. Net churn averaged **0.1% per month in 2025**, down from 0.2% in 2024 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — "Low Customer Churn"; Item 5]`. Cohort revenue keeps building: monthly ARPAC rises from US$1.3 in month 1 to US$30.7 by month 90 `[Q2'26 deck, p.28 (appendix) — managerial basis]`. | Strong, but behaviourally rather than contractually. A further 23.4% of FY2025 total revenue (US$3,690.9m) is treasury and sovereign interest, which recurs with the Selic rate rather than with any customer `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 6(a), p.F-30]`. |
| **Customer stickiness** *(higher = better)* | **74** | Nu was the **primary banking relationship for over 62% of active customers** at 31 Dec 2025, on its own definition — at least 50% of post-tax monthly income moving out of the NuAccount in a month `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.A]`. Roughly 90% of Brazilian customers hold a deposit or Money Box balance `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5]`. | Strong on behaviour, zero on contract. The regulator is actively lowering the switching cost: under open finance Nu "is required to share standardized data related to our customers… which make it easier for other market participants to compete with us", and the filing states this "may intensify competition… enabling customers to move such financial obligations from our platform to other competing platforms" `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D — Substantial and increasingly intense competition]`. Half the margin — 10.5 percentage points at Q2'26 — is consumed by credit cost before any downturn. The pre-2023 margin swing is a scaling business emerging from losses, not a cycle, so it is not treated as cycle evidence. The honest limit: **there is no Brazilian consumer downturn inside the profitable record**, so through-cycle NIM stability is not proven from this pool. |

That is what high regulatory capital intensity looks like. Scored 45 rather than lower because the 15.7% CAR still sits 520bp above the minimum. The filing carries a standing risk factor: *"Rapid, significant and disruptive technological changes have impacted or may in the future impact the industries in which we operate"* `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D]`. The clearest evidence is Nu itself: a company that did not exist in 2012 is now the primary banking relationship for over 62% of its 118m Brazilian customers, which proves this competitive set can reorder inside a decade. The counterweight, stated fairly: lending money to consumers at a spread is an old and stable activity, and Nu's 118m-customer position will not flip in 24 months. That is why the score is 38 and not 20. See §4 for the required flag. |

Funding cost tracks the central bank: deposits are paid at 88% of the interbank rate `[Q2'26 deck, p.14 — managerial basis]`. A single rule change to one product — the FGTS *saque-aniversário* amendment effective 1 Nov 2025 — cut that product's originations *"by about 50% to 60%"* and took roughly 2–3 percentage points off one quarter's portfolio growth `[Q4 2025 Earnings Call (25 Feb 2026), CFO Q&A — tier-6 transcript]`. Four regulators are live (BCB/CMN, CNBV/Banxico, SFC) with a fifth pending (OCC conditional approval 29 Jan 2026, final approval not granted) `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 35(a), p.F-98]`. | Very weak — very high dependence, and this is the lowest row in the table. It is not a criticism of management; it is a description of where the levers sit. The register in `10_external-dependency §1A` lists thirteen named programmes and rules, of which the pool shows at least four changing terms inside the last twelve months. |
| **Commodity dependence** *(low dependence = high score)* — **bank lens: rate cycle / NIM sensitivity** | **40** | No physical commodity exposure exists: no inventory, no freight, no crop or weather-linked book, and FY2025 property-and-equipment capex of US$7.2m `[10_external-dependency §1; CIQ Cash Flow export — vendor export]`. Disclosed sensitivity: a 400bp move in the Brazilian risk-free curve is **US$(306,267) thousand** of fair value, and DV01 on that curve was US$(766) thousand at 31 Dec 2025 and US$(685) thousand at 30 Jun 2026 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 11, Interest Rate Risk; Q2 FY26 Interim Report, Note 32]`. About 23.4% of FY2025 total revenue is treasury and sovereign interest that moves with the policy rate `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 6(a), p.F-30]`. | **Transparency on the overlay:** on the generic lens (physical commodity inputs) this row would score ~85. On the bank lens it scores 40, because "the input whose price management cannot set" is the interbank rate, and both sides of the book move with it. Not lower than 40 because the exposure is genuinely two-sided — the credit book is short-dated and reprices too, which management describes as *"a particularly short duration… we can react fast"* `[Q1 2026 Earnings Call (14 May 2026) — tier-6 transcript]`. |
| **Cyclicality** *(low cyclicality = high score)* — **bank lens: credit-cycle / NPL severity** | **30** | The book is 92% unsecured: credit card 66%, unsecured lending 26%, secured only 8% at Q2'26 `[Q2'26 deck, p.13 — managerial basis]` — the most cycle-sensitive form of consumer credit. Non-performing loans as a share of total loans have risen every year: **3.9% (FY2021) → 6.0% → 6.7% → 7.8% → 7.9% → 8.6% (LTM Jun-2026)** `[CIQ Financials → Ratios export — vendor export]`; 90+ days past due was 6.9% at Q2'26, up 35bp in the quarter, while 15–90 days fell 16bp to 4.8% `[Q2'26 deck, p.17 — managerial basis]`. FY2025 ECL of US$4,204.9m was 34.8% of the Note-34 revenue base `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5; Note 34(b)]`. The company's own worst disclosed scenario is mild: a 100% downside macro weighting lifts card-and-loan ECL from US$5,021,996 thousand to US$5,403,461 thousand, **+US$381.5m (+7.6%)**, which is 13.3% of FY2025 net income `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 4 — Sensitivity analysis]`. | Weak — high cyclicality. **Adjudicating the number that disagrees (§3):** the overlay's "rising NPAs with **falling** coverage" red flag does **not** trip. Total allowance over the 90+ NPL stock ran 239%, 246%, 224%, 228%, 229%, 246%, 244%, 230%, 233%, 252%, **244%** from Q4'23 to Q2'26, and the allowance build was 113% of new 15+ day delinquency formation in Q2'26 `[Q2'26 deck, p.19 — managerial basis]`. Coverage is flat to up while the NPL ratio rises, so the rise reads as book mix and seasoning of a portfolio growing 37% FX-neutral, not as under-provisioning. The score stays at 30 anyway, because the exposure is structural: the entire profitable record (FY2023 onward) sits inside a period when Brazilian unemployment **fell** from 6.2% to 5.1% `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 macroeconomic indicators table]`. |
| **Disclosure quality** *(higher = better)* | **58** | **For:** an unusually complete market-risk suite — DV01 by curve, 400bp and 200bp standardised shocks, VaR by entity, three-weighting ECL sensitivity, and a delinquency bridge decomposed into named drivers including *"intentional risk expansions"* `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 11 and Note 4; Q2'26 deck, pp.17–19]`; a repeated explicit negative on customer concentration — no customer at 10%+ of revenue in FY2023, FY2024, FY2025 or H1'26 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 34(b); Q2 FY26 Interim Report, Note 34(b)]`; KPMG unqualified in every year FY2020–FY2025, one critical audit matter (the ECL estimate) `[FY25 Form 20-F, Report of Independent Registered Public Accounting Firm; Item 16C]`. **Against:** one reportable segment with **no segment profit disclosure of any kind** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 34, p.F-97]`; the geographic table covers only 76.6% of total revenue, with the US$3,690.9m treasury book given no country split at all `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 6(a) and Note 34(b)]`; country detail **narrowed** — Colombia, the US, Cayman, Germany and Argentina were named separately in the FY23 20-F and are now collapsed into "Other countries" `[FY23 Form 20-F (filed 19 Apr 2024), Segment information note vs FY25 Form 20-F, Note 34(b)]`; every headline operating metric is company-defined, non-IFRS and FX-neutral, and the efficiency-ratio definition was itself changed in Q4'25 (the same period reads 45.8% on the old method and 41.0% on the new) `[Q4'25 investor deck (25 Feb 2026), "Refining the Efficiency Ratio" and reconciliation appendix]`; no individual executive's pay is disclosed, only the US$91.3m aggregate `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 6.B]`. | Mixed-to-strong. Risk disclosure is better than most banks give; segment and definitional discipline is worse. **None of this is a language issue** — the filings are in English, and the two real gaps (Cayman company law on individual pay; IFRS 8 single-segment reporting) are legal-regime facts, not opacity (§27). |

---

## 2. Aggregate Quality Score

# 46 / 100 — Mixed / Average (band 41–60)

This is a judgement-weighted aggregate, not a straight average. For a deposit-funded consumer lender the factors that decide the outcome are the ones that govern whether the spread survives a bad year, so **cyclicality (14%) and margin stability (14%)** carry the most weight, followed by the franchise that produces the cheap funding — **customer stickiness (12%) and recurring revenue (10%)** — and by the two structural constraints on that spread, **regulatory capital intensity (12%) and regulatory dependence (11%)**. **Pricing power (8%), competitive intensity (6%), industry rate-of-change (5%), disclosure quality (5%) and rate-cycle dependence (3%)** are secondary: they shape the medium term but do not by themselves determine whether the next twelve months' earnings hold. Applying those weights to the eleven rows gives 46.1, rounded to 46.

**Cap and anchor checks, both performed:** (a) the §1 band anchor caps the aggregate at the **second-lowest row (30) + 20 = 50**; 46 clears it. (b) The Filter-5 trip (rate-of-change 38 ≤ 40) caps the business-quality aggregate at **65** under `MODULE_RULES.md`; 46 clears that too. Neither cap binds — the aggregate is set by the evidence, not by a ceiling. The reader can rebuild it: three rows in the 70s pull up, four rows at 40 or below pull down, and the weighting deliberately gives the downside rows the larger share.

---

## 3. Strongest Factor & Weakest Factor

| | Factor | Score | Why |
|---|---|---:|---|
| **Strongest** | Customer stickiness | **74** | Net churn of 0.1% per month in 2025 (from 0.2% in 2024), an 83.5% activity rate, and primary-banking-relationship status with over 62% of active customers `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.A and Item 4.B; Q2'26 deck, p.6]`. Held below 80 because none of it is contractual, and open finance is designed by the regulator to make leaving easier `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D]`. |
| **Weakest** | Regulatory dependence *(low dependence = high score)* | **20** | The price of 38% of revenue is capped by statute, the fee on another 14.2% is capped and set by Mastercard, the funding cost tracks the central bank at 88% of the interbank rate, one rule change halved a product's originations overnight, and US$186.4m was levied for other banks' failures `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B and Item 5; Q2'26 deck, p.14; Q4 2025 Earnings Call (25 Feb 2026)]`. |

---

## 4. Read

This is **not a durable compounder on the evidence available; it is a high-quality operator inside a cyclical, regulator-shaped industry** — a single-country, single-segment consumer lender whose book is 92% unsecured, whose flagship price is capped by law, and whose funding cost is a fixed share of whatever the Brazilian central bank sets. What is genuinely good about it is the customer franchise: 0.1% monthly churn, an 83.5% activity rate, and cohort revenue that keeps climbing to month 90. What is genuinely fragile is everything that stands between the 22.9% headline NIM and the shareholder: 10.5 percentage points of it already disappear into credit cost, and that credit cost is set by an economy management does not control.

**The headline returns are being earned near a favourable point in the cycle, and must be anchored as such.** Return on equity of 33% in Q2'26 — which the CFO called a record `[Q2 2026 Earnings Call (13 Aug 2026), CFO prepared remarks]` — and a risk-adjusted NIM of 12.4%, the highest in the six-quarter series, were produced with Brazilian unemployment at 5.1%, improved from 6.2% a year earlier `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 macroeconomic indicators table]`, and with the managerial loan-to-deposit ratio pushed from 50% to 58% in three quarters `[Q2'26 deck, p.16]`. **A through-cycle level cannot be computed from this pool**: the entire profitable record runs FY2023 to H1'26, and contains no Brazilian consumer downturn at anything like the current book size — the FY2021 and FY2022 losses (ROE −6.8% and −7.8% `[CIQ Ratios export — vendor export]`) were a scaling business, not a credit cycle. So the high return and the low cyclicality score are not in conflict; they are the same fact seen twice. A 22.9% NIM on an unsecured book **is** the compensation for loss risk, and the company's own worst disclosed scenario — a 100% downside macro weighting, +US$381.5m of ECL, 13.3% of FY2025 net income `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 4]` — is a weighting shift, not a recession, so it should not be read as the floor. Downstream moat and valuation work should treat 30%+ ROE as a peak-of-favourable-conditions number, not a normalised one.

**The one factor a buyer should keep their eye on over the next 24 months: risk-adjusted NIM, read alongside the 90+ NPL ratio and the Tier 1 ratio in the same quarter.** These three have been moving in a way that only holds together while conditions stay benign — risk-adjusted NIM 9.5% → 12.4% in two quarters, 90+ NPL 6.9% and rising 35bp in the quarter, Tier 1 16.2% → 14.4% → 13.4% over eighteen months `[Q2'26 deck, pp.15–17; CIQ Ratios export — vendor export]`. The falsification test is dated and cheap: the Q3 2026 results, due November 2026. Coverage is the one series that has *not* deteriorated (244% of 90+ NPL, allowance build 113% of new formation `[Q2'26 deck, p.19]`), and that is the fact to watch break first.

Because the industry rate-of-change row scored **38 (Weak, ≤40)**, this thesis is closer to a **sector / technology-cycle bet on Brazilian digital financial services than to a durable-compounder holding** (`CLAUDE.md` §24, Filter 5): the rails (Pix), the data rules (open finance), the price ceilings, the capital regime and the asset class (virtual assets) have each been rewritten inside six years, and Nu's own rise from nothing to 118m Brazilian customers is the proof that this competitive set reorders faster than a compounder thesis assumes. Per `MODULE_RULES.md` this caps the business-quality aggregate at 65 (not binding at 46), flags the thesis type to the synthesis, and discounts moat durability.

RF-BQ-005 (fast-changing industry: rate-of-change ≤40)

## business-model / 08_competitive-map.md

_Source: `08_competitive-map.md`_

# Competitive Map — NU

**Upstream inputs read:** `02_business-identity.md` and `03_segment-map.md` (both present; also read `00_data-triage.md` and `05_customer-geography.md` for regime and geography). Nothing is missing.

**Regime, standard, currency (carried from triage `00`):** US SEC foreign private issuer — the annual filing is Form 20-F, not a 10-K. IFRS as issued by the IASB. Reporting currency US dollars. Fiscal year ends 31 December. Peers are Brazilian and Mexican issuers reporting in BRL / MXN under IFRS or local bank rules; every cross-currency peer figure below carries its source and basis.

**Not used as evidence:** `data/NU/NU_Holdings_Deep_Dive_15_Page_Memo_30_Aug_2026.pdf` is a prior engine output carrying its own verdict — a §4 tier-9 user note. It was not read for and did not inform any conclusion here.

---

## 1. Dominant Segment

Nu reports **one** operating and reportable segment — Banking, meaning the whole Group, 100% of revenue and 100% of profit `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 34, p.F-97]`. Inside that single segment the only quantified split is geographic, and **Brazil is 91.4% of the Note-34 revenue base in FY2025 and 90.9% in H1 FY2026** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 34(b), p.F-97; Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), Note 34(b), p.43]`. On product, interest on credit cards and personal loans was 77.6% of that base in FY2025 and 80.3% in H1'26 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 6, pp.F-30–F-31; Q2 FY26 Interim Report, Note 6, p.16]`.

**So the peer set that matters is Brazilian consumer credit — credit cards and unsecured personal lending — with Mexico and Colombia as the growth markets, not the anchor.** Carrying the upstream qualifier intact: profit by country is **not disclosed at all**, so this dominance read rests on revenue, and roughly a fifth to a quarter of group income (treasury income) sits outside the geographic table entirely.

---

## 2. Named Competitors

**How the list was built.** Nu names its own competitors, by segment, in the Risk Factors of its annual filing:

> "Our main competitors in the Brazilian consumer credit space include Itaú Unibanco S.A., Banco Bradesco S.A., Banco Santander (Brasil) S.A., Caixa Econômica Federal and Banco do Brasil S.A. In the Brazilian investment segment… Banco BTG Pactual S.A., Banco Inter S.A., Banco C6 S.A. and XP Inc. In the Brazilian payments space… MercadoPago Instituição de Pagamento Ltda., PicPay Instituição de Pagamento S.A., PagSeguro Digital Ltd. and StoneCo Ltd., among others."
> `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D Risk Factors — "Substantial and increasingly intense competition within our industry may harm our business…", pp.107–108]`

The three profiled below are the ones that compete in the **dominant** space (Brazilian consumer credit), are of comparable scale or are the closest same-model rival, and carry public profitability so the `moat` agent has a real anchor. Nothing here is invented.

**A definition warning the `moat` agent must carry.** Every peer margin below is computed on the **Capital IQ bank template's "LTM Total Revenue"**, which for a bank is revenue *after* interest expense and loan-loss provisions. Upstream established this for Nu itself: CIQ shows FY2025 Total Revenue of US$6,991.2m where the 20-F's Note-34 base is US$12,083.8m `[03_segment-map.md §1a; Capital IQ Financials → Income Statement export (NU) — vendor export]`. All eleven companies in the comps workbook sit on that same template, so the margins are **matched-basis against each other** — but they are **not** comparable to the 20-F's own revenue line. On the filing's own base, Nu's FY2025 net margin was 23.8% (net income US$2,871.7m ÷ Note-34 revenue US$12,083.8m), not 42.7% `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5; Note 34(b), p.F-97]`.

---

### Competitor A — Itaú Unibanco Holding S.A.

- **Ticker / listing:** BOVESPA:ITUB4 (São Paulo); ADR NYSE:ITUB. Reports in Brazilian reais.
- **Where they compete:** Brazilian consumer credit — the dominant segment — plus cards, deposits, investments and wholesale banking. Brazil-wide, branch-based, all income tiers. Named by Nu in both the consumer-credit and (through subsidiaries) investment lists.
- **Scale:** LTM total revenue **US$27,593.8m** (CIQ bank basis), LTM income-statement filing date 5 Aug 2026 — **3.3× Nu's US$8,442.1m** on the identical basis `[Capital IQ Comps → Financial Data (Nu Holdings comp set), as-of 2026-08-29, USD — vendor export]`. Total credit portfolio R$1.5 trillion at 2Q26 `[Itaú Unibanco 2Q26 results press release, SEC Form 6-K, filed Aug 2026 — peer filing, read via web, not in this data pool]`.
- **Profitability / return on capital:** LTM **net income margin 32.58%** `[Capital IQ Comps → Operating Statistics, as-of 2026-08-29 — vendor export]`. Return on tangible book **24.3%**, my arithmetic on the vendor's own per-share figures (LTM diluted EPS US$0.81 ÷ LTM tangible book value per share US$3.34) `[Capital IQ Comps → Financial Data, as-of 2026-08-29 — vendor export; arithmetic mine]`. Cross-check from the company's own disclosure: **recurring managerial ROE 24.3% annualised in 2Q26**, 24.5% for 1H26, on recurring managerial profit of R$12.4bn in the quarter `[Itaú Unibanco 2Q26 results press release, SEC Form 6-K, filed Aug 2026 — read via web, dated, peer filing not in this pool]`. Two different measures (LTM return on tangible book vs quarterly recurring managerial ROE on average equity) landing on the same number is corroboration, not one figure counted twice. CET1 12.3% at 2Q26; 90-day NPL 1.9% for a sixth straight quarter `[same 6-K]`.
- **Source named in:** Nu's own annual filing `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D Risk Factors, pp.107–108]`; also in the CIQ competitor export, named there by third parties `[Capital IQ Competitors export (NU), as-of Aug-2026 — vendor export; rows sourced to PicS N.V. 2026 Form 20-F and Inter & Co 2026 Form 20-F]`.
- **One-line read:** The largest private incumbent and the benchmark the market actually measures Nu against — on the Q2'26 call a JPMorgan analyst put Nu's >US$1bn quarterly net income directly against Itaú's retail operation at "around $1.1 billion" `[Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript, 13 Aug 2026, Q&A]`.

### Competitor B — Banco Bradesco S.A.

- **Ticker / listing:** BOVESPA:BBDC4 (São Paulo); ADR NYSE:BBD. Reports in Brazilian reais.
- **Where they compete:** Brazilian mass-market and mid-market retail banking — current accounts, credit cards, payroll and personal loans — plus a large insurance arm. Brazil-wide and branch-heavy. This is the incumbent whose customer base overlaps Nu's target segment most directly.
- **Scale:** LTM total revenue **US$17,909.9m** (CIQ bank basis), filing date 5 Aug 2026 — **2.1× Nu** `[Capital IQ Comps → Financial Data, as-of 2026-08-29 — vendor export]`. Expanded loan book R$1.137 trillion, total assets R$2.5 trillion at 2Q26 `[Bradesco 2Q26 results presentation, Aug 2026, as summarised by Investing.com, 2026 — web source, dated, unverified]`.
- **Profitability / return on capital:** LTM **net income margin 26.07%** `[Capital IQ Comps → Operating Statistics, as-of 2026-08-29 — vendor export]`. Return on tangible book **15.2%** (US$0.44 EPS ÷ US$2.90 tangible book per share) `[Capital IQ Comps → Financial Data — vendor export; arithmetic mine]`. Company-reported cross-check: **ROAE 16.2% in 2Q26** on recurring net income of R$7.1bn, up 16.2% year on year `[Bradesco 2Q26 results presentation, Aug 2026, via Investing.com — web source, dated, unverified]`. The two reads differ by 1.0 percentage point, which is what you expect between an LTM tangible-book measure and a quarterly average-equity measure; neither is a Nu filing and both are stated at their own tier.
- **Source named in:** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D Risk Factors, pp.107–108]`; also in the CIQ competitor export against Nu Pagamentos, named by Inter & Co and PicS in their own 20-Fs `[Capital IQ Competitors export (NU), as-of Aug-2026 — vendor export]`.
- **One-line read:** Roughly twice Nu's revenue and roughly half its return on tangible book — the clearest single illustration of the cost gap Nu says it is exploiting.

### Competitor C — Inter & Co, Inc.

- **Ticker / listing:** NasdaqGS:INTR (also B3 BDR). Brazilian digital bank, US-listed, reports in Brazilian reais.
- **Where they compete:** Brazil, digital-only retail banking — the same branchless model as Nu, across accounts, cards, credit and investments, plus a US business. Named by Nu in the *investment* list rather than the consumer-credit list, so the overlap with the dominant segment is real but partial `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D Risk Factors, pp.107–108]`.
- **Scale:** LTM total revenue **US$1,279.1m** (CIQ bank basis), filing date 5 Aug 2026 — about **one-seventh of Nu's US$8,442.1m** `[Capital IQ Comps → Financial Data, as-of 2026-08-29 — vendor export]`. Total assets passed R$100bn for the first time in 2Q26 `[Inter & Co 2Q26 results, SEC Form 6-K, Aug 2026 — peer filing, read via web, not in this pool]`. **Vendor conflict flagged:** the CIQ Competitors export lists Inter's LTM revenue as US$158.6m for the same 30 Jun 2026 date `[Capital IQ Competitors export (NU) — vendor export]`. That cannot be reconciled with a 22.9% net margin and R$421m of quarterly profit; the comps workbook figure of US$1,279.1m is the one that ties, and it is the one used. Do not use the US$158.6m.
- **Profitability / return on capital:** LTM **net income margin 22.92%** `[Capital IQ Comps → Operating Statistics, as-of 2026-08-29 — vendor export]`. Return on tangible book **16.7%** (US$0.66 EPS ÷ US$3.95 tangible book per share) `[Capital IQ Comps → Financial Data — vendor export; arithmetic mine]`. Company-reported cross-check: **ROE 16.3% in 2Q26**, record net income of R$421m (about US$81m), up 34% year on year, net revenue +32% `[Inter & Co 2Q26 results, SEC Form 6-K / press release, Aug 2026 — read via web, dated, peer filing not in this pool]`.
- **Source named in:** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D Risk Factors, pp.107–108]` (as Banco Inter S.A.); and three separate rows of the CIQ competitor export, one of them sourced to Nu's own 2026 Form 20-F `[Capital IQ Competitors export (NU), as-of Aug-2026 — vendor export]`.
- **One-line read:** The same branchless model at one-seventh the size and roughly half the return on tangible book — evidence that the digital format alone is not what produces Nu's returns.

### Also named in the dominant segment, profiled in brief

Kept short because they are either state-controlled, unlisted, or compete mainly outside Brazilian consumer credit. All are named in Nu's own filing at the citation above. Margins and returns on the same matched CIQ basis, with return on tangible book my arithmetic on the vendor's EPS ÷ tangible book per share.

| Competitor | Listing | LTM revenue (US$m, CIQ bank basis) | LTM net income margin | Return on tangible book (derived) |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Banco Santander (Brasil) S.A. | BOVESPA:SANB11 | 9,271.3 | 28.66% | 14.7% (0.71 ÷ 4.83) |
| Banco do Brasil S.A. (state-controlled) | BOVESPA:BBAS3 | 14,406.0 | 21.83% | 9.6% (0.55 ÷ 5.71) |
| Banco BTG Pactual S.A. | BOVESPA:BPAC11 | 8,780.8 | 39.80% | 23.7% (0.72 ÷ 3.04) |
| Caixa Econômica Federal (state-owned, unlisted) | — | 14,819.3 (to 31 Mar 2026) | **Not public / not disclosed** | **Not public / not disclosed** |
| Banco C6 S.A. (unlisted) | — | 2,743.5 (to 31 Dec 2024) | **Not public / not disclosed** | **Not public / not disclosed** |
| PicPay Instituição de Pagamento S.A. (unlisted) | — | 1,214.2 (to 31 Dec 2022 — stale) | **Not public / not disclosed** | **Not public / not disclosed** |

Sources: revenue, margin and per-share figures `[Capital IQ Comps → Financial Data and Operating Statistics (Nu Holdings comp set), as-of 2026-08-29, USD — vendor export]`; Caixa, C6, PicPay and Mercado Pago revenue and their LTM dates `[Capital IQ Competitors export (NU), as-of Aug-2026 — vendor export]`. Note the LTM dates: the C6, PicPay and Mercado Pago figures are one to four years old and must not be treated as current.

**For the `moat` agent, the comparison line, all on the identical CIQ bank basis as of 29 Aug 2026:**

| | Nu Holdings | Itaú | Bradesco | Inter & Co | Peer median (comp set of 10) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| LTM total revenue (US$m) | 8,442.1 | 27,593.8 | 17,909.9 | 1,279.1 | 8,532.3 |
| LTM net income margin | **42.73%** | 32.58% | 26.07% | 22.92% | 27.37% |
| Return on tangible book (derived) | **29.2%** | 24.3% | 15.2% | 16.7% | — |
| LTM revenue growth, 1 yr | **44.33%** | 6.63% | 6.23% | 25.42% | 8.07% |

`[Capital IQ Comps → Financial Data and Operating Statistics, as-of 2026-08-29, USD — vendor export; return on tangible book is my arithmetic on the vendor's EPS ÷ tangible book per share]`. Nu's own disclosed figure is **ROE 33% in Q2'26**, described by the CFO as a record `[Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript, 13 Aug 2026, CFO prepared remarks]` — higher than the 29.2% derived here because it is a quarterly annualised return on total equity, not an LTM return on tangible book. Use one basis or the other, not both.

---

## 3. Competitive Position

**Gaining share — but the rate of gain has slowed sharply, and that qualifier travels with the verdict.** Nu's share of Brazilian credit card purchase volume ran **8.7% (2021) → 11.6% (2022) → 13.9% (2023) → 14.2% (2024) → 14.7% (2025)**, per the card-industry association ABECS `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — "Rapid growth of our consumer credit business and associated credit loss provisioning", p.164]`. Read the increments, not the level: +2.9pp, +2.3pp, then **+0.3pp and +0.5pp**. The direction is up; the pace in the last two years is roughly a fifth of what it was in the first two. A separate ABECS read puts Nu at **16% of total card transaction volumes in Q4'25** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Cards, p.46]`.

Two supporting reads, both pointing the same way. First, category share: digital banks' share of total outstanding loans to individuals in Brazil went from **below 1% in 2018 to above 8% at September 2025**, and above 14% excluding earmarked loans such as mortgages and rural credit `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.22 — Nu's internal analysis of BCB data]`. Second, payments position: Nu's share of Pix transfer originations reached **close to 20%** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Pix, pp.45–46, citing BACEN]`.

**The number that cuts the other way, named.** Management itself says Nu holds only **"7% market share of that profit pool"** in Brazil, against a gross profit pool it sizes at about US$100 billion `[Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript, 13 Aug 2026, Q&A]`, and the 20-F puts group market share at "approximately 5% of SAM" for FY2025 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.20 — internal estimates]`. Both are company estimates, not audited figures. So a 14.7% share of card purchase volume does not translate into a 14.7% share of the money the industry earns — Nu is a large share of the transaction flow and a small share of the profit. On the outer markets: Mexico card purchase-volume share **surpassed 5%** in 2025 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.46, per Banxico]` and Colombian customers went from ~800k (Dec 2023) to 1.2m (Dec 2024) to over 1.7m (Dec 2025) `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.46]` — both gaining from a very low base.

**One disclosure caveat on the headline share figure.** The 20-F sentence reads "our share of the Brazilian card PV market (including credit, debit and prepaid) has risen steadily each year. In 2025, we represented 14.7% of the total credit card PV" — the parenthetical says all three card types, the sentence says credit cards only. They are different denominators (2025 credit card PV US$563bn; debit and prepaid US$251bn) `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.46, per ABECS]`. I read 14.7% as the credit-card figure because that is what the sentence states, and because the separate Q4'25 all-card figure is given as 16%. The ambiguity is the filing's, not a gap in the data, and the conservative reading is used.

---

## 4. Competitive Shape

**Consolidated — a five-firm oligopoly in which Nu is the largest challenger and is still taking share, though at a decelerating rate.** The company's own filing gives the supporting fact: the **five largest incumbent banks in Brazil, Mexico and Colombia hold on average between 68% and 80% of all loans and deposits across all segments**, per those countries' central banks as of 31 December 2025 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.A — Our Attractive Opportunity, p.19]`. In Colombia the same filing is more precise: five banks hold 72% of credit card purchase volume and 73% of retail deposits `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.29, per SFC]`; in Mexico the five largest hold close to 85% of the credit card industry's portfolio `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.46]`.

**The one measure in Nu's own filing that argues the other way, addressed by name.** The 20-F cites a Herfindahl–Hirschman Index — the standard 0–10,000 concentration measure — of **889 for Brazil**, 993 for Mexico and 1,262 for Colombia, against the United States at 328, Germany 373 and France 488 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.21, per BCB, CNBV, SFC and the ECB, 2024–2025 data]`. Nu presents 889 as evidence of concentration because it is nearly three times the US figure. On the conventional antitrust reading, 889 sits **below** the 1,000–1,500 line usually treated as the start of moderate concentration — so on that yardstick Brazilian banking is not concentrated at all. The two facts are arithmetically compatible: five roughly evenly sized firms at about 13–14% each produce a top-five share near 70% and an HHI near 900. The honest description is therefore **a top-heavy market with several similarly sized large players rather than one dominant firm** — which is exactly the shape that lets a challenger take 6 points of card share in four years without any incumbent collapsing. The cost gap Nu claims is the mechanism: it states the main Brazilian incumbents each run between 1,685 and 3,955 branches and service points and between 49,661 and 85,206 employees `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.21]`, against Nu's branchless model and an efficiency ratio of 19.5% in Q2'26 on the company's own non-IFRS definition `[Q2 FY26 investor deck, 13 Aug 2026, slide 6]` — a company-defined measure that, as upstream flagged, is **not** the conventional cost-to-income ratio and is not directly comparable to an incumbent's.

---

## 5. Caveat

Credible competitors were identified, so the selection limitation does not apply. Four limits on what this map can prove:

1. **No peer filing is in the data pool.** The pool is Nu-only. Every peer number here comes from either the Capital IQ comps workbook (a §4 tier-5 vendor export) or a dated web read of the peer's own recent results release. The Itaú, Bradesco and Inter return figures are labelled as such at each use. Resolution: add the 2Q26 results filings of ITUB, BBDC4 and INTR to the pool so peer returns can be cited at tier 1–2.
2. **Return on tangible book is derived, not disclosed.** For every listed peer I computed it as the vendor's LTM diluted EPS ÷ LTM tangible book value per share, which is matched-basis across the whole comp set including Nu. It is not an ROIC and not a filed ROE. Where the company's own ROE is available (Itaú 24.3%, Bradesco 16.2%, Inter 16.3%, Nu 33%) the two reads agree within about one point for the peers, but they are different measures over different periods and must not be mixed in one table.
3. **Nu's 42.7% net margin is on the vendor's post-provision revenue base, not the filing's.** On the 20-F's own Note-34 revenue the FY2025 net margin was 23.8%. The peer comparison is valid only because all eleven companies sit on the same CIQ bank template. Any claim that Nu "earns more than peers" must state that basis or it is not testable.
4. **Two competitor figures in the pool are stale or wrong.** The CIQ Competitors export carries LTM revenue dates as old as 31 December 2022 (PicPay) and 31 December 2023 (Mercado Pago), and it reports Inter & Co at US$158.6m against the comps workbook's US$1,279.1m for the same date. Do not use the Competitors export as a source of size for any private or subsidiary rival without a second read.

Sources used from outside the data pool, listed for audit: [Itaú Unibanco 2Q26 results press release (SEC Form 6-K)](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0001132597/000113259726000215/eng_pressreleasex2t26.htm) · [Itaú Unibanco 2Q26 filing summary](https://www.stocktitan.net/sec-filings/ITUB/6-k-itau-unibanco-holding-s-a-current-report-foreign-issuer-40d198ec1ced.html) · [Bradesco Q2 2026 results slides summary](https://www.investing.com/news/company-news/bradesco-q2-2026-slides-loan-growth-accelerates-profit-extends-streak-93CH-4843653) · [Inter & Co 2Q26 results (6-K summary)](https://www.stocktitan.net/sec-filings/INTR/6-k-inter-co-inc-current-report-foreign-issuer-cbebe00f932e.html).

## business-model / 09_moat.md

_Source: `09_moat.md`_

# Moat — NU

**Upstream inputs read:** `08_competitive-map.md` (required — present), `07_business-quality.md`, `10_external-dependency.md`, `06_value-chain.md`, `04_unit-economics.md`, `03_segment-map.md`, `02_business-identity.md`. Nothing is missing. No competitor is named here that competitive-map did not name.

**Regime and basis.** Nu Holdings Ltd. (NYSE: NU, Class A ordinary shares, USD) is a Cayman-incorporated US SEC foreign private issuer; the annual filing is **Form 20-F**, not a 10-K. **IFRS as issued by the IASB**, reporting currency **US dollars**, fiscal year ends 31 December `[FY25 Form 20-F (filed 8 Apr 2026), cover page]`.

**Sector overlay: bank / lender.** Per the triage, the Capital IQ workbook uses a **Bank template**, so there is **no EBITDA, no EBIT and no gross-margin row** anywhere in it. This report therefore tests returns the way a bank must be tested — **return on equity (ROE) against the cost of equity**, not ROIC against WACC — and builds the competitive economics on bank measures (net income margin, return on tangible book, net interest margin, efficiency). Forcing ROIC/WACC onto a deposit-funded lender would be a category error.

**Three bases are kept apart throughout.** (a) **IFRS / audited** — total revenue, expected credit loss, net income, equity, capital ratios. (b) **Managerial / non-IFRS, FX-neutral** — NIM, risk-adjusted NIM, ARPAC, cost to serve, efficiency ratio; company-defined, restated in Q4'25, not equal to the audited figures. (c) **Vendor (§4 tier 5)** — Capital IQ ratio and comps series, labelled at every use. Every number below says which basis it is on.

**Not used as evidence.** `data/NU/NU_Holdings_Deep_Dive_15_Page_Memo_30_Aug_2026.pdf` is a prior engine output carrying its own moat verdict. It is a §4 tier-9 user note; it was not read for, and did not inform, anything here.

---

## 1. Named Competitors

Inherited verbatim from `08_competitive-map.md §2`, which built the list from Nu's own Risk Factors `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D — "Substantial and increasingly intense competition within our industry may harm our business…", pp.107–108]`.

**Profiled peers (the ones that compete in the dominant segment — Brazilian consumer credit — and carry public profitability):**

- **Itaú Unibanco Holding S.A.** (BOVESPA:ITUB4 / NYSE:ITUB) — the largest private incumbent; LTM revenue US$27,593.8m, 3.3× Nu on the matched vendor basis; branch-based, all income tiers.
- **Banco Bradesco S.A.** (BOVESPA:BBDC4 / NYSE:BBD) — mass-market and mid-market retail incumbent, branch-heavy; the customer base that overlaps Nu's target segment most directly; roughly 2.1× Nu's revenue.
- **Inter & Co, Inc.** (NasdaqGS:INTR) — the same branchless digital model at about one-seventh of Nu's revenue; the cleanest test of whether "digital" alone produces Nu's returns.

**Also named in Nu's filing, profiled in brief by competitive-map:** Banco Santander (Brasil) S.A. · Banco do Brasil S.A. (state-controlled) · Caixa Econômica Federal (state-owned, unlisted — profitability not public) · Banco BTG Pactual S.A. · Banco C6 S.A. (unlisted, not public) · XP Inc. · MercadoPago (MELI subsidiary, not public) · PicPay (unlisted, not public) · PagSeguro Digital Ltd. · StoneCo Ltd.
