## 2. Moat Sources

Bands (`CLAUDE.md` §12): 0–20 very weak · 21–40 weak · 41–60 mixed · 61–80 strong · 81–100 very strong. Higher = better in every row.

| Possible Moat | Present? (Y/N) | Evidence | Strength /100 |
|---|---|---|---:|
| **Brand** | **Y** | Net Promoter Score (a 0–100 satisfaction measure: the share of customers who would recommend the product minus the share who would not) of **64 for Nu against an incumbent average of 43**, per a Deloitte PRIMS survey quoted in the filing `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.—, "Incumbents scored a low average of 43 in NPS… Nu currently leads the market with 64"]`. In Colombia the same measure is **1.65× the incumbent average** (Bain rNPS Prism) and in Mexico "around 15pts above the average NPS for incumbent banks" `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B]`. It converts into money: **71% of all new Brazilian customers in 2025 were sourced organically** through word-of-mouth and the member-get-member referral programme, and **80–90% per year since inception** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — "Our existing customers are core to our marketing"; Item 4.B — MGM]`, which is why cost to acquire a customer is **US$7.4** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — "Low Cost to Acquire", p.157]`. Legally protected: in October 2024 INPI, Brazil's industrial-property authority, granted NUBANK **"highly-renowned" brand status**, giving exclusivity across all classes and market segments, a status the World Intellectual Property Organization also recognises `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.D — Intellectual Property]`. | **72** |
| **Cost advantage** | **Y** | The strongest row, and it is measured on both sides of the spread. **Operating cost:** Nu estimates its cost to serve and general-and-administrative expense per active customer is **about 85% lower than Brazilian incumbents'**, computed off those incumbents' own published FY2025 accounts; **14,314 customers per employee against an incumbent average of 1,234** — 11.6× `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — "Low Cost to Serve" and "High Cost to Serve"]`. The incumbents carry **1,685–3,955 branches and service points and 49,661–85,206 employees each** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.21]`. The company-defined efficiency ratio fell **50% (Q2'22) → 30% → 26% → 21% → 20% (Q2'26)**, and 21.4% → 19.5% across the last six quarters `[Q2'26 investor deck (13 Aug 2026), pp.6 and 22 — managerial, non-IFRS]`. **Funding cost:** deposits are paid at **88% of the local interbank rate, three percentage points less than a year earlier**, and all US$41.9bn were "acquired directly, without the use of third-party brokers or intermediaries" `[Q2'26 deck, p.14 — managerial; FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — "Low Cost of Funding"]`. **Two honest limits:** the 85% figure is the company's own estimate, not an audited or third-party number, and the efficiency ratio is a company-defined measure — **not** the conventional cost-to-income ratio, and its definition was changed in Q4'25 (the same period reads 45.8% old / 41.0% new) `[Q4'25 investor deck (25 Feb 2026), "Refining the Efficiency Ratio"]`. | **78** |
| **Distribution** | **Y** | Nu owns the entire channel: one app, no branches, no agents, no deposit brokers, no loan intermediaries `[06_value-chain §1, citing FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 and Item 4.B]`. That is what lets it undercut on price — the central bank's December 2025 rate statistics put Nubank among the **lowest payroll-loan rates in Brazil**, which the filing attributes to not paying a distributing intermediary `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — payroll loans]`. Scored only 60 because the channel itself is copyable: Inter & Co, C6 and Mercado Pago run the same branchless model, and Inter earns roughly half Nu's return on tangible book on it `[08_competitive-map §2, Competitor C]`. Being direct is table stakes for a digital entrant; being direct *at Nu's scale* is the part that is hard, and that is scored in the next row. | **60** |
| **Scale** | **Y** | **113 million Brazilian customers at 31 Dec 2025 — about 62% of the country's population aged 18 and above** — and Brazilian customer growth in 2025 that "outperformed the five largest incumbent banks combined", making Nu the largest private financial institution in Brazil by customer count on BCB data `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — "A Digital Financial Services Leader"]`. 139m group customers and US$45.3bn of deposits at Q2'26 `[Q2'26 deck, pp.6 and 14 — managerial]`. Scale is what converts the fixed-cost technology platform into the 85% cost gap and what makes the deposit base self-funding. **The number that cuts the other way, named:** management's own estimate is that Nu holds only **"7% market share of that profit pool"** in Brazil against a roughly US$100bn gross pool, and the 20-F puts group share at "approximately 5% of SAM" `[Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), Q&A — tier-6; FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, p.20 — internal estimate]`. Nu is a large share of the *transactions* and a small share of the *profits*, so scale here is a cost-side asset, not a profit-pool position. Hence 68, not higher. | **68** |
| **Technology / IP** | **N** *(as a legal moat)* | Stated plainly in the filing: "As of December 31, 2025, we did **not own any Brazil-issued patents or registered copyrights**." One US patent granted (the ultraviolet card feature, USPTO, 25 Jan 2022) and one still pending (NuTap, filed 11 May 2022) `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.D — Intellectual Property]`. Protection rests on trademarks and trade secrets, not on patents. The platform is real — an all-digital cloud architecture, GenAI customer service, six new data sources taking underwriting to fifteen `[Q2'26 deck, pp.10–11]` — but it runs on **third-party** infrastructure the company says it "cannot easily switch" away from (AWS) `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D]`, which is a dependency, not an owned asset. The economic benefit of the technology is already counted in the cost-advantage row; counting it again as IP would double-count it. | **25** |
| **Licenses / regulation** | **Y, weak** | Real licences exist and a non-financial entrant cannot skip them: **Nu Pagamentos** (BCB-authorised payment institution), **Nu Financeira** (BCB-authorised credit, financing and investment company — an SCFI), Nu Investimentos (securities), Nu Corretora de Seguros (SUSEP) `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B — Regulatory Overview, Brazil]`; a **Mexican banking licence** granted 24 Apr 2025, operating authorisation notified 9 Jul 2026, began operating as a bank **6 Aug 2026** `[Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), Note 35(a)]`; a Colombian regulated-entity licence `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B]`; a US OCC charter that is **conditional only — final approval not granted** `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 35(a)]`. Scored 35 because the regulator is on the *other* side of this ledger more than Nu's: Nu does **not** hold a Brazilian banking licence and had to agree on 20 Jul 2026 to buy Banco Porto Real simply to keep the word "bank" in its name `[Q2 FY26 Interim Report, Note 35(b)]`; open finance forces it to share its own customers' data with rivals; Pix and the payment-institution regime are the doors Nu itself walked through. A regime that let this entrant in cannot be scored as a wall around it. | **35** |
| **Network effects** | **N** | No evidence of the classic effect — a customer being at Nu is not made more valuable by other customers being at Nu. The reason is regulatory and specific: **Pix, the central bank's instant-payment rail, is interoperable by law**, so transfers work identically to any other institution; Nu earns no fee on Pix at all `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5, footnote — card metrics exclude "Pix, WhatsApp payments or traditional wire transfers from which we do not earn fees"; Item 4.B — Pix]`. Nu holds close to 20% of Pix originations `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.B, pp.45–46, per BACEN]` and earns nothing from the share. What does exist is (a) a **referral loop** — 71% organic acquisition — which is a brand and cost effect, already scored above, and (b) a **data-scale effect** in underwriting (fifteen data sources feeding the credit models) `[Q2'26 deck, p.11]`, which is scale, not a network. Marked N rather than a weak Y because the pool contains no measurement of either loop's competitive effect. | **22** |
| **Switching costs** | **Y, behavioural only** | Behaviourally strong: **net churn averaged 0.1% per month in 2025** (0.2% in 2024), the activity rate is 83.5% across 139m customers, Nu is the **primary banking relationship for over 62% of active customers** on its own definition (at least half of post-tax monthly income leaving the account in a month), and roughly 90% of Brazilian customers hold a deposit or Money Box balance `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 4.A and Item 4.B — "Low Customer Churn"; Q2'26 deck, p.6]`. Contractually zero: no term, no minimum, no fee on the core card, deposits repayable on demand `[05_customer-geography §3]`. And the regulator is deliberately lowering the cost of leaving — Nu "is required to share standardized data related to our customers… which make it easier for other market participants to compete with us", and the filing says this "may intensify competition… enabling customers to move such financial obligations from our platform to other competing platforms" `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D]`. Habit is not a lock; scored mixed. | **45** |
| **Natural resource access** | **N** | Not applicable. No physical inputs of any kind — no inventory, no freight, no extraction rights; FY2025 property-and-equipment capital spending was **US$7.2m** for the whole group `[CIQ Financials → Cash Flow export, FY2025 — vendor export]`. | **0** |
| **Location advantage** | **N** | Not applicable in the physical sense — the model is defined by having no branches. The only "location" analogue is being the incumbent challenger in one national market, which is already scored under Scale; scoring it again here would double-count. Nothing in the filings evidences a site, licence-by-geography, or catchment advantage. | **10** |

**Summary:** four moat sources are present and evidenced — **cost advantage (78), brand (72), scale (68), distribution (60)** — plus behavioural switching costs (45) and weak licence protection (35). Technology/IP, network effects, natural-resource access and location are not present.

## 3. Competitive Economics

**Read the basis warning before the numbers.** Every peer figure below is taken from `08_competitive-map §2` and is **not re-sourced here**, so the two reports cannot diverge. All eleven companies sit on the **Capital IQ bank template**, where "Total Revenue" is revenue *after* interest expense and loan-loss provisions — so the margins are matched against each other but are **not** comparable to any company's own filed revenue line. On the 20-F's own Note-34 base, Nu's FY2025 net margin was **23.8%**, and on the IFRS total-revenue base (US$15,774.8m) it was **18.2%** (US$2,868.9m ÷ US$15,774.8m — my arithmetic on `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5, Statement of Income Data`), not 42.7%.

**Bank-appropriate margin comparison, same matched vendor basis (LTM to 30 Jun 2026):** Nu net income margin **42.73%** · Itaú 32.58% · Bradesco 26.07% · Inter 22.92% · peer median (comp set of 10) 27.37% `[Capital IQ Comps → Operating Statistics, as-of 2026-08-29 — vendor export]`. Nu is the highest of the eleven.

**Nu's own bank measures (managerial, non-IFRS, FX-neutral; no peer equivalent exists in this pool, so these are not a comparison):** net interest margin — the gap between what it earns on loans and what it pays on deposits — **22.9%** in Q2'26, up from 18.8% four quarters earlier; **risk-adjusted** net interest margin, the same gap after credit losses, **12.4%**, the best of the six disclosed quarters against a six-quarter average of 10.4%; company-defined efficiency ratio **19.5%** `[Q2'26 investor deck (13 Aug 2026), pp.15, 16, 22]`.

### The economic moat test

> Return on capital **above** cost of capital: **31.6% ROE (LTM to 30 Jun 2026, computed on average equity)** vs **16.51% cost of equity** (**+1,512 bps**) — cost of equity is the company's own disclosed rate: *"The discount rate used was the cost of equity for business in Brazil"*, 16.51%, with a 3.69% perpetual growth rate, used in the goodwill impairment test performed 30 September 2025 `[FY25 Form 20-F, Consolidated Financial Statements, Note 3(b) — Impairment of goodwill, p.F-38]`. **Scope label, required:** this is a **CGU-level** rate (the investments-activities cash-generating unit), not a group cost of equity, so it is used here as a **labelled comparator**, cross-checked against an independent group estimate below. It is nonetheless the closest disclosed read available, and it is the right *geography* — Brazil is 91.3% of geographically-attributed revenue `[FY25 Form 20-F, Note 34(b), p.F-97]`.

**Independent group cross-check — CAPM, *inference, not from filings*.** Inputs shown: Brazilian nominal risk-free rate **15.00%** (the Selic policy rate as of the annual-report date; average CDI interbank rate was 14.3% in 2025) `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 3.D and Item 5, macroeconomic indicators table]`; five-year beta **0.94** `[Capital IQ Comps → Operating Statistics, as-of 2026-08-29 — vendor export]`; equity risk premium **4–6 percentage points — judgment, no sourced Brazilian ERP exists in this pool**. That gives a group BRL cost of equity of roughly **18.8%–20.6%, say 19–21%**. Two honest weaknesses: the beta is measured on a USD-listed line against a US index and is being paired with a BRL risk-free rate, which is a basis mismatch; and the ERP is judgment, not evidence. **The conservative default (§4) is therefore to test against the higher independent estimate, not the company's own lower figure.** Against 21%: LTM ROE 31.6% is **+1,063 bps** above; FY2025 ROE 30.3% is **+928 bps** above. The verdict is the same either way — the spread is positive and large.

**Through-cycle, not a single peak year — the required second read, and it changes the picture.**

| Return basis | ROE | vs 16.51% (company-disclosed) | vs 21% (independent estimate) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| FY2021 −6.78%, FY2022 −7.81%, FY2023 18.24%, FY2024 28.07%, FY2025 30.28% — **five-year average 12.40%** | **12.40%** | **−411 bps (below)** | **−860 bps (below)** |
| Same five years plus LTM Jun-26 — **six-period average 15.60%** | **15.60%** | **−91 bps (essentially at)** | **−540 bps (below)** |
| Profitable era only, FY2023 → LTM Jun-26 — **four-period average 27.06%** | **27.06%** | +1,055 bps (above) | +606 bps (above) |
| **Latest — LTM to 30 Jun 2026** | **31.63%** | **+1,512 bps (above)** | **+1,063 bps (above)** |

`[CIQ Financials → Ratios export, FY2021–LTM Jun-30-2026 — vendor export]`

**How to read those four rows, because they are not in conflict.** The loss-inclusive average is dragged below the cost of equity by FY2021 and FY2022, which were a **scaling business emerging from start-up losses, not a credit cycle** — upstream established this and the qualifier travels `[07_business-quality §1, margin-stability row]`. So the five-year average understates the mature economics. But the profitable-era average overstates the through-cycle level for the opposite reason: **the entire profitable record sits inside a benign Brazilian consumer environment**, with unemployment falling from 6.2% to 5.1% `[FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5, macroeconomic indicators table]`, on a book that is **92% unsecured** (credit card 66%, unsecured lending 26%) `[Q2'26 deck, p.13 — managerial]`. **A true through-cycle ROE cannot be computed from this pool: there is no Brazilian consumer downturn in the profitable record at anything like the current book size.** The latest figure is explicitly a peak — the CFO called the Q2'26 ROE of 33% "a record", risk-adjusted NIM at 12.4% is the highest of six quarters, and the loan-to-deposit ratio was pushed from 50% to 58% in three quarters to get there `[Q2 FY26 earnings call transcript (13 Aug 2026), CFO prepared remarks; Q2'26 deck, pp.15–16]`.

**Cross-checking the management-headline return, as required.** Nu's headline is **ROE 33% in Q2'26**, a single quarter annualised on total equity `[Q2 FY26 transcript, CFO prepared remarks]`. My own computation on the standard base (net income ÷ average equity) gives **FY2025: US$2,868.9m ÷ ((7,647.1 + 11,321.6)/2 = 9,484.3) = 30.25%**, and **LTM Jun-26: US$3,607.1m ÷ ((9,576 + 13,251.7)/2 = 11,413.9) = 31.6%** `[CIQ Financials → Balance Sheet and Income Statement exports — vendor export; arithmetic mine]`. Both tie the vendor's 30.28% and 31.63% to within 0.03pp, so there is no material divergence — but the 33% headline is a **peak-quarter annualised** figure and is not used as the test number. The computed LTM and FY figures are.

**Two mechanical checks the rules require, answered:**
- **Net-cash / gross-capital adjustment: not applicable.** The rule exists to stop a collapsed, cash-netted denominator inflating a return. Here the denominator is **shareholders' equity** (US$13,251.7m at 30 Jun 2026), which is not netted against anything; the vendor's "net cash" of US$9,274.2m is an artefact of a bank's balance sheet holding customer deposits, not a capital-structure fact `[CIQ facts sidecar, net_debt_m −9,274.2 — vendor basis]`. No gross-capital restatement is needed or meaningful.
- **Normalized tax rate anchor for the DCF (`valuation/04_intrinsic-dcf` §1).** The ROE numerator is already after tax, so no NOPAT is built here — but the anchor is stated so the DCF reconciles to it. Reported effective tax rate: **FY2023 33.0% · FY2024 29.4% · FY2025 25.8% · H1'26 11.8%**. The H1'26 rate is heavily distorted and must not be used: against a statutory combined Brazilian rate of **42.5%**, the reported charge was cut by US$280.8m of "effect of different tax rates — subsidiaries and parent company", US$108.8m of interest on capital, a US$28.4m one-off deferred-tax remeasurement from the CSLL rate change (Complementary Law 224/2025), and US$253.6m of foreign tax credits, non-taxable sovereign-bond interest and tax incentives `[Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), Note — Income tax reconciliation, items (i)–(iii)]`. **The normalized structural rate to use is ~26%** — the FY2025 audited full-year rate, stripping those one-offs. Direction of error is known and stated: the enacted CSLL step-ups (payment institutions 9% → 12% in 2026–27 → 15% from 2028; SCFIs 17.5% → 20%) push the structural rate **up** from here, so 26% is a floor, not a ceiling `[same note, item (ii)]`.

---

## 4. Where The Company Sits

1. **Relative to peers:** **Nu sits at the top of its named peers on both margin and capital efficiency.** LTM net income margin 42.73% against Itaú 32.58%, Bradesco 26.07%, Inter 22.92% and a peer median of 27.37%; return on tangible book 29.2% against Itaú 24.3%, BTG 23.7%, Inter 16.7%, Bradesco 15.2%, Santander Brasil 14.7% and Banco do Brasil 9.6% — the highest of the eleven companies on the matched vendor basis `[Capital IQ Comps → Financial Data and Operating Statistics, as-of 2026-08-29 — vendor export, via 08_competitive-map §2]`. Four of the eleven (Caixa, C6, PicPay, Mercado Pago) disclose nothing, so the ranking covers the listed set only.

2. **Absolute (the economic moat test):** **the company earns a return on capital above its cost of capital — 31.6% ROE against a 16.5%–21% cost of equity (a gap of roughly +1,060 to +1,510 bps)** — which is the decisive test of whether the advantage is economic rather than merely structural. Nu passes it on the latest and on the profitable-era average. It does **not** pass it on the loss-inclusive five-year average (12.40%, −411 bps below the company's own 16.51%), and no genuine through-cycle figure exists because the profitable record contains no consumer downturn.

---

## 5. Moat Verdict

# Narrow moat

**Why not Strong.** The advantage is real, specific and measured — an ~85% lower cost to serve, 14,314 customers per employee against incumbents' 1,234, a 64-versus-43 NPS that delivers 71% of new customers free, and 62% of Brazil's adults already on the platform — and it currently produces a return roughly 1,000–1,500 basis points above the cost of equity. But "Strong" requires that spread to be **sustained through the cycle**, and it is not yet: the profitable record is three and a half years old, every one of those years ran with Brazilian unemployment falling to 5.1%, the book is 92% unsecured, and the five-year loss-inclusive average return of 12.40% sits *below* the company's own disclosed 16.51% Brazilian cost of equity. On top of that, `07_business-quality` scored the industry rate-of-change row **38 (weak, ≤40)**, tripping the fast-changing-industry filter (`CLAUDE.md` §24, Filter 5) — the rails (Pix), the data rules (open finance), the price ceiling on card interest, the capital regime and the virtual-asset regime have each been rewritten inside six years, so **moat durability is explicitly discounted here**. A moat in an industry that reorders this fast decays faster than the same moat in a boring one.

**The strongest moat is the cost advantage (78/100), and the durability test it must pass over the next five years is this:** hold a return on equity above roughly 16.5% *through one full Brazilian consumer credit downturn*, with the 90+ day non-performing loan ratio (6.9% at Q2'26, up 35bp in the quarter) rising materially, while the price of the largest revenue line stays capped by Law 14,690/2023 and open finance keeps lowering the cost for customers to leave. Nothing in this pool tests that, because nothing in this pool contains that downturn. If it clears it, this is a Strong moat; if the spread over cost of equity closes in a normal recession, the cost advantage was a cyclical margin, not a moat.

**Naming the metric that disagrees with the verdict (§3).** The single loudest number arguing for **Strong** rather than Narrow is the peer gap: Nu out-earns every listed named competitor on both measures, and by a wide margin — 42.73% net margin against a 27.37% peer median, 29.2% return on tangible book against 24.3% for the best-performing large incumbent. That is real and it is not dismissed. It does not lift the verdict to Strong for one reason: peer superiority is not the economic test, and the *duration* of Nu's superiority is three and a half years inside one favourable credit environment, against Itaú's twenty-plus-year record of mid-twenties returns. A wide gap measured over a short, benign window is a weaker claim than a narrower gap measured across cycles.

---

### Moat trajectory — **stable**

Separate from the level. The direction over the last three to five years is **stable**: the *return* spread is widening, but the two lines that measure whether the *advantage itself* is still being extended — the annual share gain and the after-credit-cost spread ratio — have both flattened, and the improvement is being measured inside a benign credit cycle.

**Trajectory evidence panel — window FY2023 (the first profitable year) to LTM/Q2 2026, with the five-year series shown where it exists.** Rows adapted to the bank template, which carries no gross-margin, EBITDA or EBIT line at all.

| Metric (same window) | Start | Latest | Direction | Supports / contradicts "stable" |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| **Gross margin** — bank analogue: IFRS gross profit ratio (revenue less funding cost, transactional expenses and expected credit loss, ÷ IFRS total revenue) | **43.48%** (FY2023); peak **45.61%** (FY2024) | **40.18%** (H1'26); 42.56% Q2'26 standalone | **Down** — −330bp from FY2023, −543bp from the FY2024 peak | **Contradicts** any "widening" read. This is the cleanest measure of the spread Nu keeps after paying for money and for bad debts, and it is thinner than two years ago. |
| **EBITDA margin** — bank analogue: pre-tax (EBT) margin on the IFRS total-revenue base | **19.2%** (FY2023: 1,539.1 ÷ 8,029.0) | **22.7%** (LTM: 4,384.6 ÷ 19,339.7) | **Up** +350bp | Supports. *(My arithmetic; IFRS revenue rebuilt as CIQ revenue-before-loan-losses + interest expense, which reconciles to the filed FY2024 11,517.1 and FY2025 15,774.8.)* |
| **EBIT / operating margin** — bank analogue: net income margin on the IFRS total-revenue base | **12.8%** (FY2023) | **18.7%** (LTM Jun-26) | **Up** +590bp | Supports. On the matched vendor (post-provision) base the same series runs 27.8% → 42.7%. |
| **Return on capital vs cost of capital** — ROE vs cost of equity | **18.24%** vs 16.51% = **+173bp** (FY2023) | **31.63%** vs 16.51% = **+1,512bp** (LTM) | **Up sharply** | Supports — but the latest point is a labelled cycle peak, and the five-year average spread is *negative* (−411bp). |
| **Market share** — Brazilian credit-card purchase-volume share (ABECS) | **8.7%** (2021) | **14.7%** (2025) | **Level up; rate of gain down** — annual increments +2.9pp, +2.3pp, **+0.3pp, +0.5pp** | **Contradicts** "widening". The level is still rising; the *rate* has fallen to roughly a fifth of what it was. With 62% of Brazilian adults already customers, the runway on this measure is visibly shorter. |
| **Cash conversion (CFO ÷ EBITDA)** | LTM cash from operations **−US$10,304.8m**; no EBITDA row exists | — | **Not meaningful** | Neither input is defined for a bank: a growing lender's loan book is an operating outflow, so cash from operations is negative by construction, and the bank template has no EBITDA. **Bank substitute — company-defined efficiency ratio: 30% (Q2'23) → 26% → 21% → 19.5% (Q2'26); 21.4% → 19.5% over the last six quarters.** Supports (cost advantage still improving, though the last-six-quarter gain is under 2 percentage points against 9 points in the prior two years — flattening). |
| **Pricing / realised price or mix** — monthly ARPAC (revenue per active customer) and NIM | ARPAC **US$9.8** (FY2023); NIM 18.8% (Q2'25) | ARPAC **US$17.1** (Q2'26); NIM **22.9%** | **Up** | **Partly contradicts.** The dollars are up, but this is **not price**: the price of 38% of revenue is capped by statute, the fee on another 14.2% is set by Mastercard and capped by the central bank, and management's own quarter-on-quarter walk attributes the NIM rise to **mix** (+152bp then +178bp from Credit Income, with the loan-to-deposit ratio moving 50% → 58%), not to charging more. And the card take rate is genuinely unsettled: +6.5% on the 17% volume base the filing attaches to card income, **−3.4%** on the 29% total-purchase-volume figure in the same paragraph. |

`[Sources, in row order: 04_unit-economics §2 and FY25 Form 20-F, Statement of Income Data, p.155 · CIQ Financials → Income Statement export — vendor export, arithmetic mine · same · CIQ Financials → Ratios export — vendor export, and FY25 Form 20-F Note 3(b) for 16.51% · FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5, p.164, per ABECS · CIQ facts sidecar ltm_ocf_m and Q2'26 investor deck pp.6, 22 — managerial · Q2'26 deck p.28 and pp.15–16 — managerial; FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5 — Total revenue and Total fee and commission income]`

**Adjudicating the panel, by name, before naming the direction.** Five of the seven usable rows point up (pre-tax margin, net margin, return-on-capital spread, efficiency ratio, ARPAC/NIM), one points down (the gross-profit ratio, −543bp from its FY2024 peak) and one is not meaningful for a bank (cash conversion). On a straight count that majority would license **widening**. It does not, for three reasons that have to be stated rather than skipped. First, the **down row is the one that measures the moat most directly**: the gross-profit ratio is what Nu keeps after paying for its money and for its bad debts, and it is thinner than it was two years ago even as the top line trebled. Second, the **share row is up in level but down in rate** — a +0.3pp and +0.5pp gain in the last two years against +2.9pp and +2.3pp in the first two, on a base where 62% of Brazilian adults are already customers; a challenger taking share five times more slowly than it was is not extending its advantage, it is consolidating one. Third, the **pricing row is mix, not price** — management's own attribution says so — and the price of the biggest revenue line is set by statute, so this row cannot be read as evidence of growing pricing power. Against that, "eroding" is plainly not supportable either: the return spread over the cost of equity went from +173bp to +1,512bp, the cost ratio keeps improving, and churn halved. **The calibrated word is therefore *stable*: the level of the moat is holding and profitability is rising, but the extension of the advantage has flattened, and none of it has been tested against a downturn.** Neither "widening confirmed" nor "erosion confirmed" may be carried downstream from this panel.

**Which way I would be wrong, and when.** The single next data point is the **Q3 2026 results, due November 2026**: specifically the risk-adjusted net interest margin read beside the 90+ day non-performing loan ratio and the Tier 1 capital ratio in the same quarter. If risk-adjusted NIM falls back toward its 10.4% six-quarter average while 90+ NPL keeps climbing from 6.9% and Tier 1 keeps sliding from 13.4%, the return spread narrows from a peak that was never through-cycle, and the trajectory read moves from **stable to eroding**. If risk-adjusted NIM holds above 12% with 90+ NPL flat or lower, the read moves to **widening** and the level verdict moves toward Strong. A second, slower check lands with the FY2026 20-F (around April 2027), which prints the next annual ABECS card-share increment: another +0.3–0.5pp confirms the share ceiling; a return to +1pp or more reopens the widening case.

---

**Caps and flags carried to `99_business-model-synthesis`:** Filter 5 (fast-changing industry) is tripped upstream at a rate-of-change score of 38 `[07_business-quality §4, RF-BQ-005]`; per `MODULE_RULES.md` this discounts moat durability, and it is the reason a return 1,000–1,500 bps above the cost of equity is classified Narrow rather than Strong. The moat verdict is **not** an erosion signal for the permanent-impairment / declining-perpetuity trigger in `valuation/04` §5 and `valuation/07` — the trajectory is stable, not eroding — but the durability period assumed in any DCF should be short, and the normalized structural tax rate the DCF must reconcile to is **~26%**, not the distorted 11.8% H1'26 reported rate.



---

## business-model / 10_external-dependency.md

_Source: `10_external-dependency.md`_

# External Dependency Check — NU

**Direction warning: the score in Section 4 is INVERTED. Higher = WORSE (more dangerous dependence on things management cannot control).**

**Regime (from triage `00`):** Nu Holdings Ltd. is a Cayman-incorporated, NYSE-listed foreign private issuer reporting under **IFRS** in **US dollars**, fiscal year ending 31 December. The annual filing is **Form 20-F** (FY2025, filed 8 Apr 2026); interim statements are the reviewed report for the three and six months ended 30 Jun 2026 (filed 14 Aug 2026). Operations are almost entirely in Brazil, with Mexico and Colombia. Functional currencies of the operating subsidiaries are BRL, MXN and COP; every cross-currency figure below carries its rate and date.

**Why concentration matters before we start.** In FY2025, Brazil produced **US$11,038.3m** of the geographically-attributed revenue of **US$12,083.8m** — **91.3%** — against Mexico US$808.1m and other countries US$237.3m [FY25 20-F, Note 34(b) Segment information]. Note 34(b) is a defined subset (interest income on cards, loans and other receivables, card income, late fees, insurance commission and other fees) and does **not** tie to IFRS total revenue of US$15,774.8m [FY25 20-F, Item 5, Total revenue]; the 20-F does not reconcile the two, so treat 91.3% as the share of the geographically-attributed measure, not of total revenue. Either way, this is a one-country business. And **85.2% of FY2025 total revenue was interest income and gains net of losses on financial instruments** — US$13.4bn of US$15.8bn — with fees at 14.8% [FY25 20-F, Item 5, "We earn revenue from two main sources"]. A single-country consumer lender earning five-sixths of its revenue from interest is, by construction, exposed to that country's rate, currency, credit and policy cycles.

---

## 1. Dependency Table

Direction reminder: this table feeds an **inverted** score. "High" dependency is bad.

| External Variable | Dependency Level | Why It Matters | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Interest rates (Brazil Selic / CDI, plus MXN TIIE, COP IBR)** | **High** | Funding reprices with the central bank's rate almost one-for-one, while the biggest asset — credit-card revolving — has its total charges capped by law. Deposits are paid at a set share of the interbank rate: **88% of the interbank rate in Q2'26**, from 91% in Q2'25. Deposits stood at **US$45.3bn**. Brazil's average CDI (the interbank rate) was **14.3% in 2025 vs 10.8% in 2024**; Selic was **15.00%** as of the 20-F date. The pool carries no Selic reading later than 8 Apr 2026 — do not assume the current level. | `Q2'26 earnings deck (13 Aug 2026), "Deposit Franchise" slide, Cost of Deposits · % of Interbank Rate`; `FY25 20-F, Item 5, macroeconomic indicators table`; `FY25 20-F, Item 3.D, "The surge of inflation…"` ("As of the date of this annual report, the Selic rate was 15.00%") |
| **FX (BRL, MXN, COP vs USD)** | **High for reported results; Low for operating cash flows** | Two different things, and they must not be blurred. Operationally the company is matched: "none of the entities in our corporate structure had significant financial exposures in a currency other than their respective functional currencies" [FY25 20-F, Item 11]; the same statement is repeated at 30 Jun 2026 [Q2 FY26 Interim Report, Note 32, FX risk]. But the **translation** of Brazilian earnings and equity into the USD reporting currency is deliberately left mostly unhedged: "We decided not to hedge our foreign exchange exposure originated by our investments in Brazil, Colombia and Mexico." A 17.8% BRL shock moves the Brazilian net investment by **US$1,369.8m**; all subsidiaries together **US$1,509.6m**, on total equity of US$11,321.6m at 31 Dec 2025 — **13.3% of equity**. | `FY25 20-F, Item 11, Foreign Exchange Rate Risk` (shock table); `Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), Note 32`; `FY25 20-F, Consolidated statement of financial position` (Total equity 11,321,562 thousand) |
| **Government policy (named Brazilian programmes)** | **High** | One rule change to one product cut that product's new lending by half. When the FGTS rules changed on 1 Nov 2025, "our originations of FGTS loans dropp[ed] by about 50% to 60%", and group credit-portfolio growth for Q4'25 was 11% quarter-on-quarter FX-neutral against **"about 13% to 14%"** without it — roughly **2–3 percentage points of a single quarter's portfolio growth removed by one government decision**. In the other direction, the Desenrola debt-renegotiation programme improved Q2'26 cost of credit by "about 5%". See §1A for the full register. | `Q4 2025 Earnings Call (25 Feb 2026), Guilherme Marques do Lago (CFO)`; `Q2 2026 Earnings Call (13 Aug 2026), Rob Livingston (CFO), prepared remarks and Q&A` |
| **Regulation (BCB / CMN / CNBV / Banxico / SFC / OCC)** | **High** | The price of the flagship product is set by statute, not by the company: Law 14,690/2023 and CMN Res. 5,112/2023 cap total interest and charges on credit-card revolving and installment credit at **no more than the original debt amount**. Prepaid interchange is capped at **0.7%** by BCB Res. 246, which the filing says "impacted our fee and commission income". A naming rule (Joint Res. 17/25) obliges the company to obtain a Brazilian banking licence just to keep calling itself a bank — which is why it agreed to buy Banco Porto Real on 20 Jul 2026. And an industry levy landed with no company decision behind it: **US$186.4m advanced to the FGC on 25 Mar 2026** under an emergency recapitalisation plan following other banks' liquidation. | `FY25 20-F, Item 4.B, Regulatory Overview—Brazil` (Law 14,690/2023; CMN Res. 5,112/2023; BCB Res. 246; Joint Res. 17/25; FGC plan); `Q2 FY26 Interim Report, Note 35(b)` (Banco Porto Real); `Q2 FY26 Interim Report, other assets note (iv)` (US$185,488 thousand FGC advance at 30 Jun 2026) |
| **Consumer credit cycle (Brazil / Mexico / Colombia)** | **High** | Five-sixths of revenue is interest on consumer credit, and provisions are the swing item. Expected credit loss (ECL — the provision the accounting rules make it set aside for loans it expects to go bad) on cards and loans was **US$5,022.0m** at 31 Dec 2025; on a 100% downside macro weighting it would have been **US$5,403.5m**, i.e. **+US$381.5m (+7.6%)**. That single scenario shift is 13.3% of FY2025 net income of US$2,868.9m. Brazil's own inputs moved against the company in 2025: GDP growth 2.3% (from 3.4%), though unemployment improved to 5.1% (from 6.2%). | `FY25 20-F, Note 4 (Critical accounting estimates), Sensitivity analysis`; `FY25 20-F, Item 5, macroeconomic indicators table`; `FY25 20-F, Item 5, Total revenue` |
| **Sovereign / country risk and geopolitics** | **Mid** | Named, but not quantified anywhere in the filing. The 20-F carries a dedicated risk factor that "any further downgrading of Brazil's credit rating could depress the trading price of our Class A ordinary shares", and describes an "uncertain fiscal adjustment plan" with the government having "yet to present a credible fiscal adjustment plan". Tariffs and sanctions appear only inside a generic list of international-operating risks ("potential tariffs, sanctions, fines or other trade restrictions") with no exposure attached. So: real for the share price, not proven as a cash-flow driver. | `FY25 20-F, Item 3.D` ("Any further downgrading of Brazil's credit rating…"; "The current economic environment in Brazil of high interest rates…"; bulleted international-operations risks) |
| **Industrial / business cycle** | **Low** | The company reports as **one segment** and lends to consumers and micro/small businesses; there is no disclosed corporate or industrial loan book whose value would track an industrial cycle. It does serve 6.8 million small businesses, so the exposure is not zero — but it is not sized anywhere in the pool. | `FY25 20-F, Note 34, Segment information` ("The CODM considers the whole Group as a single operating and reportable segment"); `Q2 2026 Earnings Call (13 Aug 2026), David Vélez, prepared remarks` (6.8m small businesses) |

**Rows dropped as not applicable, rather than scored "Low" by default:** *commodity prices*, *freight / logistics rates*, and *weather*. This is a digital bank with no physical goods, no inventory, no transport, and no crop or weather-linked book; the FY2025 20-F discloses no commodity position in either the trading or banking book beyond the definitional mention in Item 11, and capital expenditure was **US$7.2m** for the whole of FY2025 [`CIQ Financials Segments export (NU), Capital Expenditure FY2025` — vendor export]. Brazil's economy is commodity-linked and that reaches the company second-hand through the BRL and through employment, but nothing in the pool sizes that channel, so it is captured inside the FX and consumer-cycle rows rather than invented as its own.

---

No BCB/Ministry release for the 2026 round exists in this pool |

Management frames it as helping adoption, not as a cost | Implementation deadline: end of 2026 (company-stated) | `Q2 2026 Earnings Call (13 Aug 2026), David Vélez, prepared remarks` — §4 tier-6 transcript; no Banxico circular in this pool |

**No programme in this register has been described using "cliff", "expiry", "ends" or "withdrawn".** Two candidates were checked specifically: the FGTS scheme **continues on tightened terms** (a reduction, with both sets of terms above), and Desenrola is **company-stated as running into Q3'26**. Where a 2026 primary source is absent — Desenrola's current round, the income-tax exemption, and the Banxico June-2026 rules — the row says so, and the dependency is carried at its last established terms.

| Others (Olivia IP, Nu Tecnologia, Nuplat) | Brazil / Uruguay | 26,282 | 10–17.8% | 3,750 |
| **Total** | | **9,082,370** | | **1,509,598** |

Against total equity of **US$11,321,562 thousand** at 31 Dec 2025, that total shock is **13.3% of equity**. `FY25 20-F, Item 11, Foreign Exchange Rate Risk`; `FY25 20-F, Consolidated statement of financial position`

**(e) Credit-loss sensitivity to the macro scenario weighting, 31 Dec 2025 (US$ thousands).**

| | Weighted average (as booked) | 100% upside | 100% base | 100% downside |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Credit card and loan ECL | 5,021,996 | 4,708,093 | 4,957,850 | **5,403,461** |

The downside case is **+US$381,465 thousand (+7.6%)** versus the booked figure — 13.3% of FY2025 net income of US$2,868.9m. `FY25 20-F, Note 4, Sensitivity analysis`; `FY25 20-F, Item 5`

**(f) What the currency actually did to reported growth, both ways.** This is the cleanest measurement of FX dependency in the pool, and it cuts in opposite directions in consecutive years:

- **FY2025:** net income attributable to shareholders grew **+45.5% as reported** but **+50.6% FX-neutral** — currency cost about **5 percentage points** of reported growth. Adjusted net income: **+39.2% reported vs +44.1% FX-neutral**. [`FY25 20-F, Item 5, Adjusted Net Income reconciliation table`]
- **Q2 2026:** IFRS total revenue was **US$5,513.2m vs US$3,668.5m** a year earlier, **+50.3% as reported**, while the company's own FX-neutral **gross revenue** growth was **+39%**. The two measures are not identical — the deck's gross revenue for the quarter is c. US$5,316m against IFRS total revenue of US$5,513.2m — so the roughly 11-percentage-point gap is mostly currency but is not a clean like-for-like. The BRL closing rate was **R$5.1617/US$1.00 at 30 Jun 2026**, against an FY2025 average of **R$5.6**. [`Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), consolidated statement of income`; `Q2'26 earnings deck (13 Aug 2026), Gross Revenue slide and FX Neutral Measures methodology`; `FY25 20-F, Item 5, macroeconomic indicators table`]

**(g) A rate stress the company does not publish — computed here, and labelled as a bound.** Under **§9**, a stress with zero management response is a bound, not a forecast. Bound: a **300 bp** rise in Brazilian rates, applied to deposits of **US$45.3bn** paid at **88% of the interbank rate**, is `0.03 × 0.88 × 45.3bn ≈ **US$1.20bn** of extra annual interest expense` before any asset repricing. That is an upper bound and almost certainly wrong as a forecast, for a reason the filings support: the asset book is short-dated, so it reprices too. Management: the portfolio has "a particularly short duration, which means that if we ever did see unexpected asset quality movements, we can react fast"; and products have "very short-term duration, which gives us a huge amount of ability to react quickly". The company discloses no measure of how much of a rate move it has historically recovered, so no realised offset can be computed from this pool — the honest answer is that the true figure sits somewhere well below US$1.20bn and cannot be pinned down here. `Inference from disclosed inputs, not a company-disclosed sensitivity.` Inputs: `Q2'26 earnings deck (13 Aug 2026), Deposits and Cost of Deposits slide`; `Q1 2026 Earnings Call (14 May 2026)`; `Q2 2026 Earnings Call (13 Aug 2026), David Vélez`

---

## 3. Classification

**Partly externally driven** — material exposure, with management levers that the filings show actually working.

The case for a harsher verdict is strong and should be stated first. This is a **one-country** business (91.3% of geographically-attributed revenue from Brazil), earning **85.2% of revenue as interest**, funding itself at a contractual **88% of whatever the central bank sets**, selling a flagship product whose **total charges are capped by statute**, in a market where a single rule change halved one product's new lending overnight and a government renegotiation programme moved a quarter's cost of credit by 5%. None of those five facts is within management's control.

What keeps it out of "Mostly externally driven" is that the pool contains measured evidence of the company absorbing and offsetting these shocks, rather than merely narrating them:

- **Substitution after a policy shock.** When FGTS lending was cut by rule, the loss "was more than offset by the growth in public consignado" — a different product, same balance sheet [`Q4 2025 Earnings Call (25 Feb 2026), CFO`].
- **Funding cost managed down while rates were high.** Cost of deposits fell from **91% of the interbank rate in Q2'25 to 88% in Q2'26**, i.e. the spread widened *against* the external rate rather than passively tracking it [`Q2'26 earnings deck (13 Aug 2026)`].
- **Management's own attribution of a good quarter, which it did not have to volunteer.** Of the Q2'26 risk-adjusted-margin beat versus plan, "about 1/3 of that benefit… is coming from Desenrola. But 2/3 are coming from" the company's own credit performance and the ramp of balances written in prior quarters [`Q2 2026 Earnings Call (13 Aug 2026), CFO`]. A roughly 2:1 own-action-to-policy split is the closest thing in this pool to a measured mitigation rate.
- **Share is small enough that growth need not come from the cycle.** "We have 7% market share of that profit pool. So we're still a small player in that big market, and we get to cherry pick our customers" [`Q2 2026 Earnings Call (13 Aug 2026), David Vélez`]. A company taking share from a 7% base can grow while its end market does not.

Set against that, one lever is explicitly declined. On the currency, management states it "decided not to hedge" the translation exposure on its Brazilian, Colombian and Mexican investments [`FY25 20-F, Item 11`]. That is a choice, not a constraint — but for a USD-quoted shareholder the consequence is indistinguishable from an uncontrolled exposure, and it is the single largest number in the whole sensitivity set.

---

## 4. External Dependency Risk Score

# 57 / 100 — **INVERTED: higher = worse**

Band 41–60: *material external exposure, mixed mitigation.* This is consistent with the "Partly externally driven" classification, which spans the lower and middle bands.

How the number is built:

| Driver | Direction | Weight in the score |
|---|---|---|
| One country, 91.3% of geographically-attributed revenue; one reportable segment | Worse | Largest single push upward — nothing diversifies the shock |
| 85.2% of revenue is interest income; funding at 88% of the interbank rate | Worse | Rate cycle passes through the liability side almost mechanically |
| Flagship product's price capped by statute (Law 14,690/2023) | Worse | Removes the main pricing lever on the largest revenue line |
| Policy shocks demonstrated at 2–3pp of a quarter's portfolio growth (FGTS) and 5% of a quarter's cost of credit (Desenrola) | Worse | Measured, not hypothetical |
| Translation FX unhedged by choice: 13.3% of equity on the company's own 17.8% BRL shock | Worse | Explicitly not mitigated |
| Substitution across products after the FGTS change "more than offset" the loss | Better | Proven lever, same quarter |
| Cost of deposits improved 91% → 88% of the interbank rate over five quarters | Better | Spread managed against the external rate |
| Two-thirds of the Q2'26 margin beat attributed to own actions, one-third to policy | Better | Roughly 2:1 own-action-to-policy ratio |
| 7% profit-pool share; short-duration book allows fast repricing | Better | Growth need not come from the cycle |
| Operating FX matched — "no significant unhedged FX exposures" at both 31 Dec 2025 and 30 Jun 2026 | Better | Cash flows are insulated even though reported USD figures are not |

The score is not lower because none of the mitigations touch the two biggest exposures — the currency (declined) and the statutory price cap (impossible). It is not higher because the company has repeatedly demonstrated, with numbers, that it offsets policy shocks within a quarter or two.

---

## 5. The Single Biggest Lever

**The Brazilian real against the US dollar.** A 20% adverse move (BRL depreciating) would cut reported USD revenue from Brazil — US$11,038.3m, 91.3% of the geographically-attributed total — by roughly a sixth, and the company's own table puts a 17.8% BRL shock at **US$1,369.8m** on the Brazilian net investment alone, 12.1% of total equity, on an exposure it has explicitly chosen not to hedge. Nothing else in the disclosures is that large: the 400 bp Brazilian rate shock is US$306.3m of fair value, and the full downside credit scenario is US$381.5m of extra provisions. The distinction matters and should not be lost — the underlying Brazilian business would be untouched in local currency, so this is damage to the reported USD figures and to the value of the NYSE-listed line a dollar investor actually holds, not to the operations.



---

_Source: `11_capital-allocation-governance.md`_

# Capital Allocation & Governance — NU

**Company:** Nu Holdings Ltd. (NYSE:NU, Class A ordinary shares) · Cayman Islands incorporation, operations in Brazil / Mexico / Colombia · **US SEC foreign private issuer**, so the annual filing is Form 20-F, not a 10-K, and there is no DEF 14A (CLAUDE.md §27).
**Reporting standard:** IFRS Accounting Standards as issued by the IASB. **Currency:** US dollars; financial-statement figures are in thousands of USD unless marked otherwise. **Fiscal year:** ends 31 December.
**Evidence base:** the supervisor-frozen generation only. All filing citations below are read from that frozen copy and labelled `data/NU/...`.

---

## 1. Signal Table

Severity is INVERTED — **higher score = worse**.

| Signal | Observation | Evidence | Severity /100 *(higher = worse)* |
|---|---|---|---:|
| Acquisition pattern (frequency, size, integration outcomes; serial-acquirer + opportunity cost — Filter 4) | Cash spent on acquisitions over five years is trivial — US$114.5m (2021), US$10.3m (2022), nil (2023), US$5.6m (2024), US$1.5m (2025) — and the one pending deal, Banco Porto Real de Investimentos S.A. (announced 20 Jul 2026), is a banking-licence purchase the company states adds no extra capital or liquidity requirement, with the price not disclosed. | `CIQ Financials→Cash Flow export, FY2021–FY2025 + LTM Jun-30-2026, "Cash Acquisitions" row — vendor export`; `Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), Note 35(b) Subsequent events — Acquisition of Banco Porto Real`; `CIQ Transaction Summary M&A/Private Placements export, 10-year history to Aug-2026 — vendor export` | 15 |
| Net share count trajectory (buybacks minus issuance, dilution) | Shares issued and fully paid went 4,818,658,640 (31 Dec 2024) → 4,855,673,632 (31 Dec 2025), +0.77%, then **fell 0.51% to 4,830,688,659 by 30 Jun 2026** as 40,659,600 shares repurchased plus 4,083,323 withheld for employee taxes outweighed 19,696,826 vested options and RSUs; 228,167,482 shares (4.7% of the current count) remain reserved for share-based payments. | `Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), Note 31 (Equity) — share tables at 30 Jun 2026 and 30 Jun 2025` | 30 |
| Dividend policy & coverage | The company has never declared or paid a cash dividend and has adopted no dividend policy, so 100% of the US$2,868.9m FY2025 profit was retained — defensible while return on equity (the profit earned on each dollar of shareholders' money) is 30.3%, but there is no stated framework telling holders when that changes. | `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 8.A — "Dividend and Dividend Policy"` ("We have never declared or paid any cash dividends"); `CIQ Financials→Cash Flow export, FY2021–FY2025, "Total Dividends Paid" row = nil every year — vendor export`; `CIQ Financials→Ratios export, FY2025 Return on Equity 30.3% — vendor export` | 25 |
| Capex intensity vs depreciation (growth vs maintenance) | FY2025 investment spend of US$340.8m (US$7.2m property and equipment plus US$333.6m intangibles, mostly software) ran at 3.5× the US$98.0m depreciation-and-amortisation charge and 4.9% of US$6,991m revenue, so nearly all of it is growth spend rather than replacing worn-out assets. | `CIQ Financials→Cash Flow export, FY2025 — "Capital Expenditures" −7.2, "Purchase/Sale of Intangibles" −333.6, "Depreciation & Amort., Total" 98.0 — vendor export`; `CIQ Financials→Segments export, 12 months Dec-31-2025 revenue 6,991 — vendor export` | 20 |
| Debt level and trajectory (absolute + vs EBITDA) | Borrowings (excluding customer deposits) rose from US$2,097.5m (Dec-2024) to US$5,277.2m (Dec-2025) to US$5,896.7m (Jun-2026) against equity of US$13,251.7m — a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.44× — while Tier 1 capital (the regulator's core loss-absorbing cushion) slipped from 16.2% of risk-weighted assets in FY2024 to 14.4% in FY2025 and 13.4% at LTM Jun-2026; **no EBITDA line exists** for this bank template, so leverage-to-EBITDA is not computable and must not be manufactured. | `CIQ Financials→Capital Structure Summary export, FY2024 / FY2025 / 3 months Jun-30-2026, "Total Debt Outstanding" — vendor export`; `CIQ Financials→Balance Sheet export, "Total Equity" 13,251.7 at 2026-06-30 — vendor export`; `CIQ Financials→Ratios export, "Tier 1 Capital Ratio %" — vendor export`; `ciq_facts.json: ltm_ebitda_m = unknown` | 30 |
| Related-party transactions | Disclosed related-party balances are immaterial — a single US$926 thousand other-liability at 31 Dec 2025 (US$1,795 thousand at 31 Dec 2024), being a cash incentive received from a company whose CEO is one of Nu's directors — and credit issued to directors and key staff is stated to be on the same terms as for unrelated customers; aggregate director and key-management pay was US$91.3m in FY2025 (US$96.0m FY2024, US$60.1m FY2023), and 0.0% of the vendor relationship graph's 11 disclosed trading rows are intra-group. | `FY25 Form 20-F, Note 28 (Related parties), a) and b)`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 6.B — Executive Compensation` (US$91.3m aggregate); `GENERATION_ROOT/relationships.json → concentration: intragroup_row_share_pct 0.0, 11 rows, 12 named counterparties, 9 third_party / 3 group / 0 likely_group — CIQ Suppliers & Customers export (NU), retrieved 2026-09-06, vendor export; scope quoted verbatim: "Recently disclosed customers only (within the last two years)", "Recently disclosed suppliers only (within the last two years)", "Include Suppliers for:Current subsidiaries"` | 8 |

| Promoter share pledging *(if applicable, e.g. Indian listings)* | **Not applicable** — this is a Cayman-incorporated NYSE foreign private issuer, so there is no SEBI-style promoter-pledge disclosure regime; no pledge or encumbrance of Vélez's or Rua California Ltd.'s shares is disclosed in the beneficial-ownership table, and the insider-trading policy permits (but restricts) pledging by employees, banning it in the 15 days before earnings. | `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 7.A beneficial-ownership table and footnotes (2) and (4)` (no pledge noted); `FY25 Form 20-F, Exhibit — Insider Trading Policy, "Pledging or margin accounts for Nubankers"` | 0 |
| Auditor history (changes, qualifications, key audit matters) | KPMG Auditores Independentes has audited the company since 2018 with an **unqualified opinion in every year FY2020–FY2025**, there has been no auditor change, the single critical audit matter is the expected-credit-loss estimate on US$5,021,996 thousand of loan-loss allowances, and non-audit work is 7.2% of total fees (US$226.8k of US$3,150.0k in FY2025). | `FY25 Form 20-F, Report of Independent Registered Public Accounting Firm — "We have served as the Company's auditor since 2018" and "Critical Audit Matters"`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 16C — Principal Accountant Fees and Services`; `CIQ Auditors export, FY2020–FY2025, Opinion = Unqualified in all six years — vendor export` | 10 |
| Restatements / accounting policy changes | No company restatement is disclosed — the 20-F cover-page error-correction checkbox is not triggered, a clawback policy is in force, and management and the auditor both concluded internal control over financial reporting was effective; the "Restated" (FY2021) and "Reclassified" (FY2022, FY2024) column labels in the vendor workbooks are Capital IQ's own normalisation tags, not company restatements. | `FY25 Form 20-F, cover page (error-correction checkboxes) and Item 15 — Controls and Procedures`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Exhibit — Compensation Recovery (clawback) Policy`; `CIQ Financials→Balance Sheet export, column headers "Restated Dec-31-2021" / "Reclassified Dec-31-2022" — vendor export` | 12 |
| Off-balance-sheet items | The material off-balance-sheet exposure is **US$28,841,327 thousand (US$28.8bn) of unused credit-card limits at 31 Dec 2025, up from US$17.7bn a year earlier** — disclosed, not hidden, and already carried inside the expected-credit-loss model — alongside small legal provisions of US$30.9m and possible-loss contingencies of about US$8.9m (US$4,372k civil + US$4,532k labour). | `FY25 Form 20-F, Note 30 (Risk management) — "Unused limits (i)" 28,841,327 / 17,663,606, with note (i) stating they are not recorded in the statement of financial position but are included in the ECL measurement`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Note 25 (Provisions and contingent liabilities), a)–c)` | 30 |
| Working capital trend (receivable days, inventory days, cash conversion) | Receivable days, inventory days and cash conversion are not meaningful for a deposit-funded lender — operating cash flow is negative by construction (LTM Jun-2026 −US$10,304.8m) because the loan book is growing, funded by US$45,328m of deposits — so the bank-appropriate read is asset quality, and that is drifting the wrong way: non-performing loans rose from 6.7% of loans (FY2023) to 7.8% (FY2024), 7.9% (FY2025) and **8.6% at LTM Jun-2026**, with the loan-loss allowance up to US$6,612.9m. | `ciq_facts.json: ltm_ocf_m −10,304.8 [CIQ Financials→Cash Flow, LTM 12 months Jun-30-2026]`; `CIQ Financials→Ratios export, "Nonperforming Loans / Total Loans %" FY2023–LTM Jun-2026 — vendor export`; `CIQ Financials→Balance Sheet export, "Allowance For Loan Losses" −6,612.9 at 2026-06-30 and "Total Deposits" 45,328.4 — vendor export` | 35 |
| Senior management turnover (CEO, CFO, board chair in last 3 years) | The founder has been chairman and CEO throughout, but three of the most senior seats changed inside about twelve months: **CFO Guilherme Lago handed over to Rob Livingston (ex-Visa) effective 13 July 2026** after five years, staying on as special advisor to the management team *and to the Audit and Risk Committee*; a new Chief Technology Officer started 18 August 2025; and former Central Bank of Brazil president (2019–2024) Roberto Campos Neto joined in July 2025 as Executive Vice-Chairman and Global Head of Public Policy — a regulator-to-regulated move worth naming, whatever its legality. | `CIQ Key Developments export, 1 Jun 2026 — "Nubank Announces CFO Changes" — vendor export`; `Q2 FY26 investor deck (13 Aug 2026), cover page (Rob Livingston as CFO)`; `FY25 Form 20-F, Item 6.A — Management Team` (Eric Young CTO from 18 Aug 2025; Campos Neto joined July 2025, "President of the Central Bank of Brazil between 2019 and 2024") | 35 |

**Acquisition-pattern test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4) — result: filter does NOT trip.** Over the full five-year public history the company spent US$131.9m of cash in total on acquisitions (2021–2025 combined), against FY2025 revenue of US$6,991m and equity of US$13,251.7m at 30 Jun 2026 — roughly 1% of a single year's revenue across five years. There is no serial-acquirer pattern, no debt-funded deal near or above the company's own value, and no evidence in the pool of businesses divested under deal pressure. Goodwill has not grown (US$409.4m at Dec-2025 versus US$414.3m at Dec-2024, still mostly the 2021 Nu Investimentos purchase at US$348.3m allocated), which is what an absence of deal-making looks like on the balance sheet [`FY25 Form 20-F, consolidated statement of financial position and Note 19 (Goodwill)`; `Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), Note 19 — "Acquisition of Nu Investimentos 348,276"`]. The pending Banco Porto Real purchase is a licence acquisition whose price is not disclosed — that single undisclosed figure is the one genuine gap in this row, and it is why the severity is 15 rather than lower. The rejector-filter cap therefore does not apply.

**On the buyback specifically.** The board approved a US$1.0bn, twelve-month repurchase programme on 3 June 2026 (expiring 3 June 2027), and by 30 June 2026 had bought 40,659,600 Class A shares for US$500,393 thousand — an average of **US$12.31 per share**, against a last close of US$14.30 on 29 Aug 2026. The shares sit in treasury and none has been cancelled; US$499,607 thousand remained available [`Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), Note 31(e) — Share repurchase program`; `ciq_facts.json: current_price 14.3 (CIQ Comps→Financial Data, as-of 2026-08-29)`]. That US$500.4m is 26% of the US$1,932.3m profit earned in the same six months. Buying below the later market price is a fact, not a verdict — one half-year is far too short a record to call it a habit.

---

## 2. Classification

**Owner-operator discipline** — with two named structural caveats that stop this being a clean sweep.

What supports the classification: acquisitions are near-zero and there is no serial-acquirer pattern; the share count has just turned **down** (−0.51% in H1 2026) after years of low-single-digit creep; related-party dealings are US$0.9m against US$6,991m of revenue; the auditor has been KPMG since 2018 with six straight unqualified opinions and only 7.2% non-audit fees; there is no restatement; contingent legal exposure is under US$40m; and the founder's 74.4% voting stake is stable, so his economics move with the minority's.

The two caveats, stated plainly rather than averaged away: (1) Nu is a **"controlled company"** under NYSE rules and so may opt out of the requirements for a majority-independent board and a fully independent compensation committee — in practice it has not needed to (8 of 10 directors are external independents, and the audit, risk and compensation committees are entirely independent), but the *option* to opt out belongs to one man and cannot be withdrawn by minority holders [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 6.C — Controlled Company Exception`; `CIQ Board Members and Committees exports, as of Aug-2026 — vendor export`]. (2) Because Cayman law does not require it, **no individual executive's pay is disclosed** — only the US$91.3m aggregate — so pay-for-performance cannot be tested at the person level [`FY25 Form 20-F, Item 6.B — "Under Cayman Islands law, we are not required to disclose compensation paid to our management team on an individual basis"`]. That is a real disclosure gap under §11; it is a legal-regime gap, not a language one, and it is not treated as opacity in the sense §27 forbids.

---

## 3. Most Material Signal

The signal to watch is **asset quality set against the capital cushion the buyback is drawing down** — the working-capital-equivalent row (severity 35) read together with the debt/capital row (30). Non-performing loans have climbed every year, 6.7% → 7.8% → 7.9% → 8.6% of loans, while the Tier 1 ratio has fallen 16.2% → 14.4% → 13.4%, and the company began returning capital (US$500.4m spent, US$499.6m still authorised) in the middle of that trend. Today none of that is alarming: return on equity is 30.3%, equity grew from US$7.6bn to US$13.3bn in eighteen months, the allowance stands at US$6,612.9m, and the buyback is a quarter of one half-year's profit. But the arithmetic of the combination is what matters — if the credit cycle turns in Brazil and the non-performing ratio pushes materially past 8.6% while Tier 1 keeps sliding, the same repurchase stops being surplus cash returned and becomes capital taken out of a thinning cushion, and the off-balance-sheet US$28.8bn of unused card limits (up 63% in one year) is precisely the exposure that draws down hardest when customers get stressed. That would move the classification from *owner-operator discipline* to *capital allocation concerns* without any single new red flag appearing. The falsification test is simple and dated: the Q3 2026 results, due November 2026, showing whether the 90-day non-performing ratio and the Tier 1 ratio move together or apart while repurchases continue.

---

## 4. Capital Allocation Score

**76 / 100** — higher = better.

Build (weights reflect what actually drives risk for a fast-growing, deposit-funded regulated lender; weighted average severity 23.8, score = 100 − 23.8):

| Signal | Severity | Weight | Contribution |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Net share count trajectory | 30 | 12% | 3.6 |
| Debt level and trajectory / capital ratio | 30 | 12% | 3.6 |
| Insider / promoter ownership and control structure | 30 | 12% | 3.6 |
| Auditor history | 10 | 10% | 1.0 |
| Acquisition pattern | 15 | 8% | 1.2 |
| Related-party transactions | 8 | 8% | 0.6 |
| Off-balance-sheet items | 30 | 8% | 2.4 |
| Asset quality (working-capital equivalent) | 35 | 8% | 2.8 |
| Capex intensity vs depreciation | 20 | 6% | 1.2 |
| Restatements / accounting policy | 12 | 6% | 0.7 |
| Dividend policy | 25 | 5% | 1.3 |
| Senior management turnover | 35 | 5% | 1.8 |
| Promoter pledging (not applicable) | 0 | 0% | 0.0 |
| **Weighted severity** | | **100%** | **23.8** |

**Rejector-filter cap (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4): NOT applied.** The acquisition-pattern row scored 15, far below the ≥70 trip threshold — five years of acquisition spend total US$131.9m of cash, there is no serial-acquirer pattern, and no deal is debt-funded or anywhere near the company's own value. The 50/100 cap therefore does not bind.

76 sits in the 61–80 "strong / low risk" band, which is consistent with the classification: a table of mostly low severities, no red flag, and two structural items (one-man voting control, no individual pay disclosure) that hold the score well below the 81–100 band.

---

### Evidence notes and limits

- **Duplication was not counted as corroboration.** The pool mirrors `Filings/` as `Filings 2/`, and several Capital IQ workbooks have "(1)" twins; every figure above is taken once, from the named source.
- **The 15-page deep-dive memo dated 30 Aug 2026 in the pool is a prior engine output**, a §4 tier-9 user note. It was not read as evidence and none of the conclusions above are drawn from it.
- **Vendor figures are labelled as vendor figures.** Where the audited 20-F carries its own number for the same item (shares outstanding, related-party balances, provisions, the buyback, unused limits), the filing's number is used and cited to the filing. Capital IQ numbers appear only where the filing does not present the series in that form (multi-year cash-flow lines, ratio history, Tier 1 ratio, insider-trade aggregation), and are cited as vendor exports.
- **`net_debt_m` −9,274.2 in `ciq_facts.json` is the CIQ vendor basis** (it nets short-term and liquid investments, a *broad* basis, not the strict total-debt-minus-cash figure) and is deliberately not used above as a headline; the debt row quotes gross debt and equity from their own sources instead (§15).
- **No EBITDA exists** on the bank template, so no leverage-to-EBITDA, interest-coverage or EV/EBITDA figure is offered.
- **Not proven from available data:** the purchase price of Banco Porto Real; individual executive compensation; any board-level statement of a capital-return framework beyond the twelve-month repurchase authorisation.

## business-model / 12_red-flags-sweep.md

_Source: `12_red-flags-sweep.md`_

# Red Flags Sweep — NU

**Upstream inputs:** all present. `00`, `01`, `02`, `03`, `04`, `05`, `06`, `07`, `08`, `10`, `11` were read in full before this sweep began. No upstream output is missing.

**Regime and basis.** Nu Holdings Ltd. (NYSE: NU, Class A ordinary shares, USD) is a Cayman-incorporated US SEC foreign private issuer; the annual filing is **Form 20-F**, not a 10-K, and there is no DEF 14A (CLAUDE.md §27). Reporting standard **IFRS as issued by the IASB**; reporting currency **US dollars**, financial-statement figures in thousands; fiscal year ends **31 December**. All filings in this pool are in English, so no translation question arises anywhere in this report.

**Not used as evidence.** `data/NU/NU_Holdings_Deep_Dive_15_Page_Memo_30_Aug_2026` is a prior engine output carrying its own verdict. Per the triage caveat it is a §4 tier-9 user note; it was not read for, and did not inform, any row below.

Severity is **INVERTED — higher score = worse.**

Meanwhile **current tax — the tax actually payable — FELL 7.3%, from US$1,536,521k to US$1,424,006k, while profit before tax ROSE 38.4%, from US$2,795,198k to US$3,868,419k.** | The reported profit that produces the 30%+ return on equity (the profit earned on each dollar of shareholders' money) is flattered by credits that cannot repeat once the deferred tax assets are used up — and Brazilian rules cap their use at **30% of taxable profit a year**. The gap between the theoretical 40% Brazilian combined tax rate and the 25.8% effective rate (29.4% in FY2024) is **US$550,621k** for FY2025 alone, roughly a fifth of net income. Its single biggest component, "Other amounts", is **US$243,158k** (up from US$161,880k) and is explained in one clause: "primarily related to the incentives and non-taxable interests on tax". ## 3. Most Severe New Flag

**The reliance on non-cash deferred-tax credits.** Over FY2023–FY2025 — which is Nu's entire profitable history — the group booked **US$1,816,376k of deferred tax benefit against US$5,874,284k of cumulative reported net income, 30.9% of the whole record**, while the tax it actually owed in FY2025 *fell* 7.3% (US$1,536,521k → US$1,424,006k) as profit before tax *rose* 38.4% (US$2,795,198k → US$3,868,419k) [`FY25 Form 20-F, Note 30 (Income taxes) a), pp.F-81–F-82`]. The synthesizer should weight this because it attacks the same number two other modules have already put weight on from a different direction: `07 §4` calls the 30%+ return on equity a peak-of-favourable-conditions figure on **credit** grounds, and `04 §3` rings the Q2'26 per-customer contribution as a peak-quarter reading. This is a third, independent and arithmetically checkable reason the reported earnings level is not a run rate — and unlike the credit argument it does not need a downturn to bite. Three disclosed facts set the direction of travel: Brazilian rules cap the use of these assets at 30% of taxable profit a year; the effective rate is 25.8% against a 40% statutory rate, a US$550,621k gap whose largest single component (US$243,158k, "Other amounts") is explained in one clause; and Complementary Law No. 224/2025 "reduced certain federal tax benefits and incentives by 10% starting in January 2026", with the filing stating the company "cannot guarantee" the rest survive [`FY25 Form 20-F, Note 30 b)`; `Item 3.D — tax risk factor`]. Carry the qualifier exactly: this is fully disclosed accounting, correctly applied, and recognising deferred tax assets is what a newly profitable company does. The flag is about the **durability** of the reported profit, not about the accounting being wrong.

`RF-RFS-001 (aggressive accounting practice pattern)` — qualifying row: **internally developed software capitalisation** (severity 52), where additions grew 84.8% against 37.0% revenue growth, US$223,167k of cost stayed off the FY2025 income statement (5.8% of pre-tax profit, up from 4.2%), and 19.5% of the year's additions were written off in the same year.

---

## 4. Cross-Cutting Patterns

**Pattern one — cost is being moved off the income statement in three separate places at once.** Internally developed software (US$223,167k net of amortisation kept off FY2025 profit), credit-card issuance costs including printing, packing and shipping ("deferred expenses… amortized based on the card's estimated useful life methodology"), and the US$185,488k FGC levy sitting in other assets [`Q2 FY26 Interim Report (14 Aug 2026), Other assets note (ii) and (iv), p.25`; intangibles movement table as cited above]. Each one is disclosed, each one is defensible on its own, and none is large enough alone to change a verdict. The pattern is that all three run the same way — current cash out, future expense — in a year when the reported profit was also carrying a US$427,259k deferred tax credit. A reader rebuilding FY2025 profit on a cash-cost basis would land materially below US$2,871,672k.

**Pattern two — the most flattering version of a metric is repeatedly the one with the least definitional discipline.** The liquidity figure quoted on the call (35%) is the lowest of three and the only one with no published definition; the efficiency ratio was redefined in Q4'25 so that the same period reads 41.0% instead of 45.8% [`07 §1`]; the LTV/CAC ratio and the "payback in under 12 months" claim were withdrawn from the FY2025 20-F with nothing put in their place [`04 §2`]; and every headline operating metric sits on a company-defined non-IFRS managerial basis that does not tie to the audited statements [`02`, `04 §head`]. No single instance proves intent, and the audited IFRS statements are clean, unqualified and reviewed. But the direction is consistent enough that the synthesizer should treat any company-defined metric in this file as requiring its definition quoted alongside it, and should not let a managerial figure stand in for an audited one.

**Pattern three — one person is the single point of failure in both directions.** The Shareholder's Agreement gives Vélez a veto over dividends, mergers, share issuance, large asset moves and executive pay that survives down to 10% of voting power (about 2.5% of the shares in issue), while the 20-F names him personally as the key-personnel risk and no succession plan is disclosed. Entrenchment and dependence are usually opposite worries; here they are the same fact.
